18.2 What the Phase I assessments did cover

Read in full, the assessments were thorough within their scope:

Site reconnaissance. Physical walkover, observation of storage, staining, containers, structures, and evidence of releases.

Regulatory database review. Federal, state and local environmental databases for the site and surrounding properties, at the radii ASTM specifies.

Historical aerial photography. 1952–1999, at intervals — the window examined in Ch. 17.

Historical topographic maps. 1942–1988, including the 1948 USGS sheet.

Recognised environmental conditions, identified and pursued. The assessments found specific issues and recommended specific follow-up:

Condition identified Recommendation
Used-oil release at the Field Office Sampling
Two removed 12,000-gallon underground storage tanks Sampling
~500-gallon UST at Cow Camp Sampling
Buried equipment and waste scraps Sampling
Former pistol ranges Lead and copper sampling (FEIR 589 MM 4.14-11)
Established Fact

This is a Phase I working correctly. It identified point sources from documentary and visual evidence and recommended targeted sampling at each. The methodology is not in question.

18.3 Agricultural land use — considered, and reasoned about

Former agricultural use was recognised. The Oso Grande assessment addressed it explicitly:

Although the site may have been in light agricultural use (dry farming), pesticides and/or herbicides are not suspected within the near-surface soils beneath the site. Any pesticides and/or herbicides at the site were likely mixed and/or placed at lower depths during the development of the site.

DTSC engaged with it. On 8 September 2003 the department required a Preliminary Endangerment Assessment. On 7 October 2003, having received an addendum, it concluded that "further environmental investigation of the site is not required."

Limitation

The reasoning was documentary and visual, not analytical. The conclusion rested on a site visit and geotechnical borings that were reviewed for methane and organics — not for pesticides or arsenic. No soil sample was analysed for either.

Note also the logic of the pesticide argument: contamination was discounted partly because surface soils "were removed prior to fill placement." That is an argument about grading, which relocates material rather than eliminating it. See Ch. 22.

18.4 The regulatory record for the footprint

Database Records for Ladera Ranch
DTSC EnviroStor 1 — Oso Grande School. Past use "AGRICULTURAL – LIVESTOCK". Status: NO CONTAMINANTS FOUND / NO ACTION REQUIRED
State Water Board GeoTracker 2 — permitted fuel tanks at two retail stations. No cleanup cases
LUST / Cortese / cleanup sites None covering the residential footprint
Limitation

"NO CONTAMINANTS FOUND" means no contaminants were sought in soil. The site was cleared without sampling. That is a legitimate outcome under the applicable process and it is not a measurement — a distinction that matters because the phrase reads, to a non-specialist, as a laboratory result.

18.5 The fair summary

Environmental review of this land happened, was documented, engaged a regulator, and identified and pursued specific contamination sources.

Its limits were the limits of its instrument and its era: a Phase I does not sample, ASTM required review only to 1940 using reasonably ascertainable sources, and the aerial archive available in 2002 began in 1952.

What follows in Ch. 19 is what does not appear in it — worded with care, because the distinction between absent from the documents obtained and never considered is the difference between a finding and an accusation.

Chapter 19

What Does Not Appear to Have Been Evaluated

The wording of this chapter is deliberate throughout, and the deliberation is the point.

"The searchable materials examined in this investigation do not appear to identify…" is not a hedge. It is the only claim the evidence supports. A document sitting in a county file cabinet, a consultant's archive, or a developer's records is invisible to this project — and must not be declared nonexistent by it.

19.1 Cattle-era land use

Established Fact

EM-023 · ESTABLISHED FACT · Confidence: High

Full-text search across every document obtained:

Term EIR 589 App. I (582k chars) 2016 PEIR (4.4M chars) Oso Grande Phase I
dip vat 0 0 0
cattle dip 0 0 0
sheep dip 0 0 —
dipping 1 — unrelated 0 0
arsenic 1 — unrelated 0 0
livestock — — 0

The two non-zero hits were checked individually. "dipping" (p. 181) is metal-finishing at an industrial facility: "dipping, and welding/cutting operations." "arsenic" (p. 119) is groundwater above the maximum contaminant level at a mine-tailings reservoir.

Neither concerns cattle.

What this means: cattle-era land use as a potential contamination source does not appear in the documents obtained. The tick eradication programme, arsenical dipping, and vat siting are absent from the searchable record examined.

What it does not mean: that the question was never considered. The Ladera Ranch entitlement EIR was not located, and its hazardous-materials appendix — identified by name — is not online.

19.2 Soil sampling across the residential footprint

Established Fact

No footprint-wide soil sampling programme appears in any document obtained. Sampling was recommended only at discrete recognised environmental conditions — a used-oil release, three underground tanks, buried scrap, and former pistol ranges.

A Phase I ESA is non-sampling by definition under ASTM E1527. The absence of sampling is a property of the instrument, not a finding about the soil.

Limitation

Tract-level grading and geotechnical reports, and any developer-commissioned sampling, are not in the public repositories searched. They almost certainly exist — mass grading requires permits, plans, and compaction testing. They have not been requested.

19.3 The specific analytical gap

This is narrower and more useful than "they didn't sample."

A sampling grid designed for field-scale agricultural residue cannot find a dipping vat.

Orchard lead arsenate and row-crop organochlorines were applied across whole fields. A grid sized to characterise field-scale application samples the thing being sought.

A dipping vat is a point source perhaps twenty metres across. A field-scale grid can pass directly over it and register nothing. Both investigations are summarised identically — "soil was sampled for arsenic on former agricultural land" — and they are not the same investigation.

Investigative Lead

The audit question is therefore not "was soil sampled?" but "was sampling ever targeted at locations where a point source would have stood?"

In the documents obtained, the answer appears to be no — sampling was targeted at documented twentieth-century point sources (tanks, oil releases, ranges), which is correct practice for the conditions the Phase I identified. No pre-1952 land-use feature was among them, because no pre-1952 imagery was reviewed.

19.4 The document that would settle this

Open Question

EM-025 · OPEN QUESTION

EIR 589 Appendix I, page 21, cites:

Michael Brandman Associates, May 1995, Environmental Impact Report for the Antonio Parkway Roadway Alignment and Land Use Plan, Appendix H — Hazardous Materials Environmental Site Assessment.

This is the hazardous-materials assessment for the land Ladera Ranch was built on.

A complete CEQAnet sweep — 22 queries with lead agency Orange County across 1990–2000, plus 14 by county across 1993–1999, every slice under the row cap — returned zero Ladera Ranch records. CEQAnet hosts no attachments for filings of that vintage.

Until this document is read, every statement in this chapter is provisional. It may address cattle-era land use in detail. It may not. Nobody outside the county currently knows.

Records request: Orange County Planning, (714) 834-2166.

19.5 The audit conclusion

On the documents obtained and read:

  1. Historical aerial review began in 1952; the quarantine ended in March 1912
  2. No reference to cattle dipping, dip vats, or arsenical dip appears anywhere
  3. No footprint-wide soil sampling programme appears
  4. The one site inside the community was cleared with no soil sampling, on documentary and visual grounds, with borings reviewed for methane and organics only

On what this does not establish:

The entitlement EIR was not located. Its hazmat appendix is not online. Grading records have not been requested. Developer-held documents have not been sought.

Limitation

This is a documented gap in the historical window, not a documented failure. The pre-1952 record was genuinely difficult to obtain in 2002 and is trivially available now.

The correct conclusion is look again — not someone should have looked harder.

Chapter 20

Agricultural Pesticides and Other Potential Exposure Pathways

A report that examines only one hypothesis is not an investigation; it is an argument. This chapter sets out every environmental pathway that has been identified as potentially relevant to this community, and grades each on the same scale — including the pathways that are better documented than the arsenical-dipping hypothesis that prompted the work.

Several of them are.

20.1 How these are ranked

Each pathway is assessed on two independent axes, which are frequently confused:

Documentation — how well established is it that this thing was present or occurred here? Plausibility of exposure — if present, how credible is a pathway to human contact?

A pathway can be well-documented and low-exposure (a plugged well a mile away), or poorly-documented and high-exposure-if-real (a dip vat under a park). Collapsing these into a single "risk" score would hide exactly the distinction that matters.

20.2 Documented: arsenic in soil at nearby school sites

Established Fact

EM-013 · ESTABLISHED FACT · Confidence: High DTSC found arsenic in soil at several school sites on former agricultural land near Ladera Ranch. Supporting: EnviroStor records for Carl Hankey Elementary, the Plant Depot School Site, and San Juan Elementary list arsenic among contaminants identified. Counter-evidence: Arsenic occurs naturally in California soils at background concentrations, and presence alone establishes neither an anthropogenic source nor any link to cattle dipping. Former orchard land carries lead arsenate residues from an entirely different practice — insecticide application to fruit trees — which is a far more common explanation for arsenic on former California agricultural land than cattle dipping. Citation: DTSC EnviroStor site records [S-ENV-01].

This is the best-documented arsenic finding in the study area, and it is important to state plainly that it does not support the dipping hypothesis over the orchard hypothesis. Lead arsenate was applied to California orchards for decades and is the default explanation for elevated arsenic on former orchard ground. Where lead and arsenic are elevated together in roughly the ratio of the compound, that is a lead-arsenate signature. A dipping-vat signature would show arsenic strongly elevated in a very small area without proportionate lead.

That distinction — the lead-to-arsenic ratio and the spatial extent — is testable, and is the single most useful discriminator available. It is developed in the recommendations chapter.

20.3 Documented: organochlorine pesticide residues

Established Fact

ESTABLISHED FACT · Confidence: High DDT and its breakdown products, chlordane, and toxaphene have been identified in soil at former agricultural sites in the immediate area. Supporting: EnviroStor records for San Juan Elementary (chlordane, DDD, DDE, DDT, plus lead and arsenic) and Ambuehl Elementary (DDD, DDE, DDT, toxaphene). Counter-evidence: These findings are at school sites on former agricultural land, not within the Ladera Ranch residential footprint. Extrapolating them to Ladera Ranch soil is not supported by any measurement. Citation: DTSC EnviroStor site records [S-ENV-01].

Organochlorines are persistent, though unlike arsenic they do degrade. Their presence confirms that this general area carries a legacy agricultural chemical burden — which is unremarkable for former Southern California farmland and is exactly what the school-site assessment programme exists to catch.

20.4 Documented: proximity to plugged oil and gas wells

Established Fact

ESTABLISHED FACT (proximity) / INVESTIGATIVE LEAD (relevance) · Confidence: High for geometry Two plugged exploratory wells lie within approximately one mile of the community centroid, one at roughly 0.25 miles. Six lie within ten kilometres. Supporting: CalGEM WellSTAR database coordinates, distances recomputed by haversine from the corrected community centroid. Counter-evidence: These are mid-twentieth-century plugged dry holes, generally a lower leakage risk than production wells. The community's drinking water is imported rather than drawn from local groundwater. Proximity is a screening signal, not a dose. Citation: CalGEM WellSTAR [S-ENV-02]; correction C-001.

This pathway deserves particular attention because it has something the dipping hypothesis lacks: a published epidemiological association with the specific cancer in question. A 2026 peer-reviewed California study reported a suggestive but non-significant association between residential proximity to abandoned oil and gas wells within 10 km and childhood Ewing sarcoma (OR 1.27, 95% CI 0.96–1.66), stronger in Hispanic children.

A confidence interval spanning 1.0 is not a positive finding, and the study's authors did not present it as one. But it is more direct evidence than exists for any other pathway considered here — and it emerged from a correction to this project's own geometry, not from looking for it.

Investigative Lead

This pathway is under-investigated relative to its evidential support. The well-integrity records, plugging documentation, and any soil-gas or methane monitoring near the two nearest wells have not been obtained. Given that this is the only pathway with a published association with Ewing sarcoma specifically, it arguably warrants higher priority than the hypothesis that prompted this investigation.

20.5 Documented practice, undocumented here: landscape pesticide application

Investigative Lead

INVESTIGATIVE LEAD · Confidence: Low Master-planned communities maintain extensive irrigated common landscaping under professional contract, and landscape maintenance is a recognised pesticide-use category in California's reporting system. Supporting: California Pesticide Use Reporting data include a landscape maintenance category. Counter-evidence and a hard limit: Analysis of the state PUR archive established that 94.6% of 79,473 Orange County records could not be located more precisely than a large grid cell, and within landscape maintenance specifically, only 22 of 15,383 records (0.1%) carried usable coordinates. The state reporting system, as it exists, cannot place applications within Ladera Ranch. This is a limitation of the data, not a finding about the community. Next step: HOA and landscape-contractor records, which are private but not necessarily inaccessible.

This is a good example of an appealing hypothesis defeated by data quality rather than by evidence. The question remains open because the instrument cannot measure it.

20.6 Plausible, undocumented: imported fill and soil redistribution

Investigative Lead

INVESTIGATIVE LEAD · Confidence: Low Mass grading redistributes near-surface soil across a development, and imported fill may originate off-site entirely. Supporting: Ladera Ranch was mass-graded; this is not in dispute and is visible in the before-and-after imagery. Counter-evidence: No evidence has been located that imported fill was contaminated, and no cut-and-fill records or import sources have yet been obtained. This is a mechanism, not a finding. Why it matters regardless of source: grading is the mechanism by which a localised point source — if one existed — could be spread across a much wider area at lower concentration. It converts a small, findable contamination footprint into a large, diffuse, and much harder one.

20.7 Present by default: natural background arsenic

Historical Context

HISTORICAL CONTEXT · Confidence: High California soils contain naturally occurring arsenic, with regional background concentrations varying by geology.

This is the null hypothesis for every arsenic finding in this investigation, and it must be defeated by evidence before any anthropogenic explanation is entertained. Any future sampling programme that does not establish local background concentrations first will be unable to interpret its own results.

20.8 Pathways assessed and not supported

Recorded because ruling things out is part of the work.

Drinking water. The community's water is imported and treated, subject to routine regulatory monitoring. No pathway is supported by available evidence.

Airborne dust from construction. Real during the active construction period and now historical. No monitoring data from that period have been located, so this cannot be assessed either way — it is unresolvable rather than ruled out.

Industrial contamination. No significant industrial facilities have been identified within the study zones that would suggest a pathway.

Recycled irrigation water. Used for common-area landscaping in the region; no evidence of contamination has been identified.

20.9 Where the evidence actually points

Setting aside the hypothesis that prompted this work and reading the evidence on its own terms:

Pathway Documentation Exposure plausibility Published link to Ewing sarcoma
Arsenic at nearby school sites Strong Moderate — off-footprint None
Organochlorines at school sites Strong Moderate — off-footprint None
Plugged oil/gas well proximity Strong Low–moderate Suggestive, non-significant
Natural background arsenic Strong Low None
Landscape pesticides Weak — unmeasurable Unknown None
Imported fill / grading Weak Unknown None
Arsenical cattle dipping None located Unknown None

The hypothesis that prompted this investigation currently sits at the bottom of this table on documentation. It has generated the most analytical work and produced the least evidence.

That is stated plainly because it is the honest reading, and because a project that quietly reordered this table to protect its founding premise would forfeit any claim to being taken seriously. The dipping hypothesis remains worth resolving — it is testable, the contamination would be persistent, and a negative result would close it permanently. But it is not currently the best-supported explanation for anything, and this publication does not present it as one.

Chapter 21

The Arsenical-Dipping Hypothesis: Evidence For, Evidence Against, and Missing Proof

This chapter states the hypothesis that prompted the investigation, assembles everything that supports it, assembles everything that weakens it, and identifies precisely what would settle it.

The counter-evidence section is longer than the supporting one. That is not a stylistic choice — it reflects the current state of the record.

21.1 The hypothesis, stated precisely

During California's cattle tick eradication era, an arsenical dipping facility operated on the land now occupied by Ladera Ranch or its immediate surroundings. Arsenic from that facility persists in soil, because arsenic does not degrade. Subsequent mass grading may have redistributed it. The 2000s residential development was built over ground whose history was never fully reconstructed, and the possibility was therefore never tested.

Stated that way, it is a chain of six propositions, each of which must hold:

  1. The tick eradication programme applied to Orange County
  2. This ranch dipped cattle, using its own facility rather than a shared or off-site one
  3. The facility stood within or near the present community footprint
  4. Arsenic residue persists at that location
  5. Development disturbed, redistributed, or built over it
  6. A pathway to human exposure exists

Proposition 1 is now ESTABLISHED. Propositions 2 through 6 remain entirely unevidenced.

USDA Bureau of Animal Industry Circular 174 (1911) lists Orange County among counties "heavily infested" with cattle ticks, south of the California quarantine line — see chapter 8. The first link holds. The remaining five do not, and the gap between "the programme reached this county" and "a vat stood here" is unbridged by any documentary evidence.

21.2 Evidence supporting the hypothesis

The land was a working cattle ranch

Established Fact

EM-001 · ESTABLISHED FACT · Confidence: High Ladera Ranch sits on land that was part of the Rancho Mission Viejo cattle operation prior to development. Supporting: USGS quadrangles from 1948 through 1968 label the area MISSION VIEJO OR LA PAZ; aerial photography from 1929 through 1946 shows open rangeland with stock trails and grazing patterns. Counter-evidence: None identified. Citation: USGS San Juan Capistrano 7.5′ quadrangle [S-MAP-01, S-MAP-04]; OC Survey historical aerials [S-IMG-01].

This establishes the precondition and nothing more. Cattle ranching was the dominant land use across most of coastal Southern California. It does not distinguish this land from thousands of square miles of it.

Arsenic is chemically persistent

Historical Context

Arsenic is an element and does not degrade. Contamination deposited during the dipping era would, in mass terms, still be present — unlike organic pesticides, which decline on a half-life.

This is what makes the hypothesis worth testing rather than dismissing on age. A 1912 organic pesticide question would be closed by chemistry. A 1912 arsenic question is not.

The terrain contains plausible facility locations

Investigative Lead

A single structure existed at 33.55505, −117.65492 on Trabuco Creek — flat valley floor, adjacent to water, at a convergence of unimproved roads. That is the physical signature of a place where cattle were handled. Counter-evidence: The map does not label it. It is equally consistent with a line camp, barn, or ranch house. Nothing distinguishes these possibilities in the imagery.

Arsenic has been found in soil nearby

Documented at several school sites on former agricultural land within a few miles. This cuts both ways and is examined as counter-evidence below, because the most likely explanation for it is not cattle dipping.

21.3 Evidence weakening the hypothesis

This section is given equal editorial weight by design, and is longer because the record currently contains more of it.

No vat has been found on imagery good enough to show one

Established Fact

The systematic 24-tile survey of Zone A at 1.15 ft/px found no vat, no corral, no pen, and no chute complex. The 1929, 1946–47, 1948, 1968, and 1974 sheets were also examined.

This was previously described as the strongest counter-evidence in the investigation. That assessment has been downgraded — see correction C-004.

Its limits, stated precisely: it is evidence against a large permanent swim-vat installation surviving to 1929. USDA Circular 174 documents that California also used cage vats (installed beside an existing corral, no drip pen, $55–65) and wade tanks (under $10, ~15 ft long). The imagery has little to no power against those designs. It is also no evidence about any facility demolished and backfilled between 1917 and 1929. Claim EM-003 now carries Low confidence.

No map labels a dipping vat

No dipping vat appears on any topographic sheet, plat, or map examined. This is weak counter-evidence, because USGS symbology had no vat symbol and such facilities were essentially never mapped — but the absence is real and is recorded.

The spatial hypothesis about development was tested and failed

Established Fact

EM-007 · ESTABLISHED FACT · Confidence: High The proposition that modern homes were preferentially built on the ranch-era water sites — where cattle concentrated, and therefore where working facilities would have stood — was tested directly and is not supported. Enrichment 0.97×, permutation p = 0.51 against a Zone-A-restricted null.

This removes a mechanism that would have connected the historical activity to the present community. It does not reduce the exposure question, since near-total development means any contamination would be near housing regardless — but the specific proposed connection is gone.

The most likely explanation for local arsenic is not cattle dipping

Lead arsenate was applied to California orchards for decades and is the default explanation for elevated arsenic on former California agricultural land. Several of the nearby school sites with arsenic findings are documented former orchard or row-crop land. A cattle-dipping explanation is not required to account for any arsenic yet measured in this area, and invoking it would be adding an entity without necessity.

No documentary evidence has been located

None of the following has been found:

  • A record that Orange County fell within the tick quarantine area
  • A record that this ranch dipped cattle
  • A purchase record for arsenical dip concentrate
  • A photograph of a vat on this ranch
  • An employee account describing dipping here
  • A map, plan, or permit for such a facility

Some of these absences reflect archives not yet searched, and searches are in progress. Others may reflect that the thing did not happen. The investigation currently cannot distinguish between these two explanations, and says so rather than treating silence as either confirmation or refutation.

Alternatives with unresolved attribution

If dipping did occur in this region, it may have been conducted at a shared, county, or railroad-siding facility rather than on individual ranches — a common arrangement, and one that would place any contamination well away from this footprint. Cattle shipped by rail were frequently handled at stockyard facilities at the siding, not on the home range.

Later investigations may have addressed this already

Environmental review was conducted for this development. Portions of it are not publicly available online. It is possible that the question was considered and resolved in documents this investigation has not obtained. Concluding otherwise from an inability to find records online would be a serious error, and this publication does not make it.

21.4 The "one large ranch" problem

A specific caution, because this is the kind of detail that becomes distorted in retelling.

Where period sources describe tick eradication activity in a region and refer to operations at an unnamed "one large ranch" or similar, that reference has not been demonstrated to be this ranch. Southern California had several very large cattle operations in this era. Treating an unattributed reference as though it identified Rancho Mission Viejo would be an inference dressed as a fact.

Open Question

Any period reference to unnamed large-ranch dipping operations in Southern California requires independent attribution before it can be connected to this land. Until the underlying document is located and read in full, no such connection is asserted here.

21.5 What would actually settle this

The hypothesis is testable. Four routes, in descending order of decisiveness.

1. Soil chemistry with the right discriminator. Targeted sampling at the Trabuco ranch node and the mapped water bodies, testing total and bioavailable arsenic together with lead. The lead-to-arsenic ratio is the key: lead arsenate from orchards produces elevated lead and arsenic in roughly compound proportion across a broad area. A dipping vat produces sharply elevated arsenic in a very small area without proportionate lead. The two signatures are distinguishable. Background must be established first.

2. Geophysics. Ground-penetrating radar or electromagnetic survey at candidate locations detects buried concrete and disturbed fill directly. This is non-destructive, comparatively inexpensive, and would answer the structural question without excavation.

3. Ranch records. Rancho Mission Viejo's own operating records. Dipping was a mandated activity that generated paperwork — inspection records, chemical purchases, correspondence with state authorities. This is now the highest-value archival lead, because the imagery avenue is close to exhausted.

4. Regulatory archives. County Agricultural Commissioner and California State Veterinarian tick-eradication files, and USDA Bureau of Animal Industry records at the National Archives. In other states, individual vats are named in state meeting minutes. This route can definitively resolve proposition 1 — whether Orange County was ever in the programme at all — and a negative answer would close the entire hypothesis.

21.6 Current assessment

Open Question

The arsenical-dipping hypothesis is UNRESOLVED and currently UNSUPPORTED BY DIRECT EVIDENCE.

It is coherent: the practice existed, the chemistry persists, the land was a cattle ranch, and the terrain contains plausible locations.

It is unevidenced: no vat, no document, no photograph, no testimony, and no measurement places it here. The strongest counter-evidence — the systematic imagery search — has real but bounded force, and the single most likely explanation for arsenic already measured in this area is a different practice entirely.

It should be neither asserted nor abandoned. It should be tested, by the methods in §21.5, starting with the archival question of whether Orange County was in the programme — which is cheap, decisive in one direction, and has not yet been answered.

The reasonable position is that this is a live question with a clear resolution path and no current answer. Anyone presenting it as more than that — in either direction — is going beyond what the evidence in this publication supports.

Chapter 22

Grading, Cut-and-Fill, and Possible Soil Redistribution

Mass grading is the mechanism that would connect a small historical contamination source to a large modern residential area. It is also the mechanism that would make such a source extraordinarily difficult to find afterwards.

This chapter sets out what is established about grading here, what is inferred, and what is unknown — which is most of it.

22.1 What is established

Established Fact

Ladera Ranch was mass-graded. This is not in dispute and is directly visible in the before-and-after imagery: the 1929–1974 frames show natural drainage-cut hillslopes; the 2022 frame shows engineered pads, benched slopes, and re-contoured drainages.

The confirming detail comes from an unexpected direction. The Oso Grande School Phase I assessment discounted pesticide risk partly on this reasoning:

Any pesticides and/or herbicides at the site were likely mixed and/or placed at lower depths during the development of the site.

and separately noted that surface soils "were removed prior to fill placement."

That is a description of soil being moved, offered as a reason not to sample. Whether it is reassuring depends entirely on where the material went — which the document does not say.

22.2 Why grading cuts both ways

The instinct is to treat grading as either burial (good) or dispersal (bad). It is neither reliably.

Dilution is real. A concentrated point source mixed through a much larger volume of fill produces lower concentrations everywhere. For a contaminant with a concentration-dependent health effect, that genuinely reduces risk at any single location.

But dilution converts a findable problem into an unfindable one. A twenty-metre vat footprint is a target. The same mass of arsenic spread across forty acres of fill at low concentration is not detectable by any sampling design anyone would think to run, because nobody would know where to run it.

And burial depth is not fixed. Material placed at depth during grading can be returned to the surface by later utility trenching, pool excavation, landscaping, or foundation work.

Limitation

None of the above is a finding about Ladera Ranch. It is a description of what mass grading does to a hypothetical point source. No contamination has been demonstrated here, so there is nothing established to have been diluted, buried, or redistributed.

22.3 The measurement that cannot be used

Buildings in this footprint sit on a median slope of 1.7° against 6.7° for available land across the same extent.

That looks like strong evidence that housing was placed on valley bottoms — where water was, and therefore where cattle were worked.

It is circular. The flatness under a house today was manufactured by the grading. The modern elevation model describes engineered pads, not the landform anyone chose between.

Limitation

EM-008 · Methodological exclusion. This figure is recorded rather than omitted because it is persuasive and will be rediscovered by anyone who runs the same analysis. It cannot support a conclusion about original siting.

What would work instead: reconstructing pre-1980 elevations from the 1948 and 1968 topographic contours, which are held by this project. That would give original landform independent of the grading. It is tractable and has not been done.

22.4 What is not known

This is the substance of the chapter, and it is mostly absence.

Question Status
Cut-and-fill volumes for Ladera Ranch Not obtained
Which areas were cut, which were filled Not obtained
Whether fill was imported from off-site Not obtained
If imported, from where Not obtained
Whether any imported fill was characterised before placement Not obtained
Depth of fill across the footprint Not obtained
Whether grading plans reference historical land use at all Not obtained
Open Question

Grading and geotechnical reports for Ladera Ranch are not in any public repository searched. CEQAnet holds no attachments for filings of that vintage; EnviroStor and GeoTracker hold one school-site record and two permitted fuel tanks between them.

These documents almost certainly exist — mass grading requires permits, plans, and compaction reports. They are held by the county, the developer, or a geotechnical firm, and they have not been requested. This is a records-access gap, not a demonstrated absence.

22.5 Why this matters for sampling design

If soil investigation is ever undertaken, grading changes the design in two specific ways.

Depth matters more than usual. Contamination that predates grading may sit under fill rather than at the surface. Surface-only sampling could return clean results over a buried source. Any programme should sample at depth in cut-and-fill transition areas, which requires knowing where those are — which requires the grading plans.

Location logic partly breaks down. The sampling frame this project produced — 41 water bodies plus one ranch structure — assumes contamination stayed where it was deposited. Grading may have moved it. The frame remains the best available starting point, because a point source is more likely to be near its origin than anywhere else, but it should not be treated as exhaustive.

Investigative Lead

Obtaining the grading plans and cut-and-fill records is a prerequisite for competent sampling design, not an optional refinement. Sampling without them risks a negative result that means nothing — clean surface soil over undisturbed fill would look identical to clean soil over nothing at all.

22.6 An honest summary

Grading is the most-invoked and least-evidenced mechanism in this investigation. It is frequently offered — including by this project, in earlier drafts — as the explanation for how a historical source could reach a modern community.

It remains a plausible mechanism with no supporting documentation whatsoever. No cut-and-fill volume, no import source, no depth profile, no plan has been obtained. Until those records are in hand, any statement about soil redistribution at Ladera Ranch is speculation with an engineering vocabulary.

A georeferenced reconstruction of the study area combining historical imagery, historical cartography, modern terrain, and modern built form — all registered to a single common extent so that any layer can be compared with any other without further transformation.

Common extent (WGS84): longitude −117.680 to −117.616, latitude 33.520 to 33.575. Approximately 5.9 km east–west by 6.1 km north–south.

Limitation

Zone A and Zone B are approximate screening aids, not legal boundaries. Zone A is a bounding box of about 5 square miles centred on the Ladera Ranch CDP centroid, against a published CDP area of 4.945 square miles. It is fit for spatial screening and is not a cadastral or jurisdictional boundary. It should never be used as one.

23.2 Georeferencing confidence, layer by layer

Different layers carry genuinely different positional confidence, and treating them as equivalent would be a mistake.

Layer group Method Confidence Residual
OC Survey aerials Rectified server-side by the county; export extent echoed and checked High (relative), Moderate (absolute) ~±25 m from export snap, plus unpublished county rectification error
USGS topographic sheets pyproj transform from GeoTIFF tags, validated against the quad neatline High Sub-pixel against the graticule
Digitised water bodies Colour threshold on the georeferenced 1968 sheet Moderate Inherits sheet error plus centroid approximation
EnviroStor / CalGEM points Agency-published coordinates High As published by the agency
OSM buildings Community-mapped centroids Moderate Completeness not guaranteed uniform

The independent check on the whole raster stack: the modern 2022 frame was exported through the identical pipeline to the identical extent. Zone A lands on the real subdivisions; Interstate 5 and Mission Viejo fall where they belong. A systematic registration error would have displaced the modern frame too. It did not.

23.3 The correction that reshaped every distance

Correction Issued

Correction C-001 — the study centroid was approximately 1.93 miles too far north.

The project inherited a working centroid of 33.5747, −117.6353. The authoritative Ladera Ranch CDP centroid is 33.5467, −117.6403.

How it was caught: rendering the map. The Zone A polygon sat visibly north of the OpenStreetMap "Ladera Ranch" place label — a contradiction that a table of coordinates would never have surfaced. This is the single strongest argument in this project for rendering spatial data rather than trusting it.

Magnitude: 1.93 miles north–south, 0.29 miles east–west.

Every distance-to-community figure in the project was recomputed. The consequences were not cosmetic:

Site Was (mi) Now (mi)
Citizens National Trust B-1 (plugged well) 2.0 0.25
O'Neill #1 (plugged well) 2.7 0.77
Blue Diamond Materials Plant 3.1 1.3
Plant Depot School Site (arsenic, nitrate) 4.8 2.9
Ambuehl Elementary (DDT, toxaphene) 5.0 3.0
San Juan Elementary (arsenic, chlordane, DDT) 5.1 3.2

Two plugged wells turned out to lie within about a mile of the community centroid, one effectively within the footprint — rather than the two to three miles previously recorded. The former-agricultural school sites carrying legacy residues are roughly three miles away, not five.

That correction is what placed this community inside the exposure contrast of the published Ewing sarcoma / abandoned-well study discussed in the alternative-pathways chapter. It came from fixing an error, not from looking for a result.

What the correction did not affect: demographics and person-years (CDP-based), the incidence scenarios and Poisson intervals, the pesticide-reporting analysis, and the PLSS finding — re-queried at the corrected point, the community still sits on unsectioned land-grant land.

23.4 The terrain analysis, and why its headline result is unusable

A drainage network was derived from the 3DEP DEM by D8 flow accumulation over a pit-filled surface, and building placement was compared against available terrain.

The measured result: buildings sit at a median slope of 1.7° against 6.7° for available land across the same extent — roughly four times flatter than the landscape average.

Why it cannot be used: Ladera Ranch was mass-graded. The pads were cut flat. The measurement describes engineered surfaces, not siting decisions relative to natural landform. It is circular with respect to the very activity under investigation.

Limitation

EM-008 · Methodological exclusion. This figure is recorded rather than omitted precisely because it is persuasive and will be rediscovered. Anyone who recomputes it should meet this note. Recovering the original landform requires reconstructing pre-1980 elevations from the 1948 and 1968 topographic contours — which is tractable, and is listed as recommended work.

A second-order caution from the same analysis: the derived channel network proved sparse relative to the visible drainage pattern (median distance to a channel of ~914 m across an area with drainages every few hundred metres), indicating the accumulation threshold was too conservative. The valley-bottom enrichment figure computed from it (1.15×) is therefore not reported as a finding anywhere in this publication. The water-proximity analysis in the water chapter, which does not depend on the derived network, is unaffected.

23.5 The interactive map

The project's web map presents these layers with the three historical rasters selectable and an opacity control for blending against a modern basemap. Water bodies are scaled by mapped surface area. Every point carries its source grade and provenance in its popup.

A note on its construction, because it is a genuine robustness lesson: the data layers were originally attached to MapLibre's load event. That event waits on sprites, glyphs, and the first tile batch — so a stalled font or sprite fetch on a slow connection would leave every research layer silently invisible over a perfectly functional basemap. A reader would see a map, notice nothing wrong, and draw conclusions from an empty overlay. Layers now attach to styledata, which fires as soon as the style is parsed.

Limitation

No patient locations, residential addresses, or case locations appear on any map layer, by permanent policy. Where a dataset would permit finer resolution than the project's privacy rules allow, the rules take precedence.

23.6 The GIS package

All layers are published as GeoJSON in WGS84, with the derived rasters and their corner coordinates, so the analysis can be reproduced or contradicted independently. Every feature carries a source_id linking to the source registry; features with no traceable source are not published.

The most useful product is not any single layer but the sampling frame: 41 water bodies plus one structure, in a single file, representing the places where the historical activity of interest would have concentrated if it occurred at all.

Chapter 24

Evidence Matrix

The complete matrix follows this chapter as a searchable, filterable section — every claim in this investigation with its classification, confidence, supporting evidence, limiting or contradictory evidence, citation, and status.

It is generated directly from research/_logs/EVIDENCE_MATRIX.csv, so the published matrix and the working data cannot drift apart.

How to read it

Four classifications, and the distinction between them does most of the work in this report:
Established Fact Documented in a primary or official source, verified against it, reproducible by following the citation
Historical Context Accurate background that situates the investigation but is not a finding about this land
Investigative Lead A testable proposition with partial support and a defined next step. Not a finding
Open Question Material, identified, and genuinely unresolved

Confidence is separate from classification. A claim can be an established fact at low confidence — EM-003, that no vat was found in the imagery, is exactly that: the observation is solid, its evidential weight is not.

The counter-evidence field is mandatory. Where a claim has no meaningful counter-evidence, that is stated explicitly rather than left blank, because an empty field is ambiguous between "none exists" and "none was sought."

What to look at first

  • EM-003 — the negative imagery finding, and why its confidence was downgraded
  • EM-007 — the spatial hypothesis that was tested and failed
  • EM-008 — a persuasive measurement recorded specifically so it is not used
  • EM-011 / EM-019 — the two claims that established Orange County was in the programme
  • EM-012 — the central open question, which remains open
  • EM-022 / EM-024 — the environmental review audit findings
  • EM-034 — 272 MB of witness testimony obtained and deliberately not interpreted

Claims are grouped by classification in Chapters 25–28. The matrix below is the complete set.

Chapter 25

Known Facts

Claims this investigation regards as established: documented in a primary or official source, verified against that source, and reproducible by a reader who follows the citation. Each carries its limiting or contradictory evidence, because an established fact with no stated limits is usually an overstated one.

27 claims in this category.

EM-001 — Ladera Ranch sits on land that was part of the Rancho Mission Viejo cattle operation prior to development

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. USGS 7.5' quadrangles 1948-1968 label the area MISSION VIEJO OR LA PAZ; OC Survey aerials 1929-1946 show open rangeland with stock trails and grazing patterns

Limiting or contradictory evidence. None identified

Citation. USGS San Juan Capistrano 7.5' quadrangle, 1948 and 1968 editions; OC Survey Historic_Imagery_v2 frames OID 346/310/293

EM-002 — The Ladera Ranch footprint was undeveloped open rangeland in every available pre-1950 aerial photograph

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Six pre-1950 frames examined (1929/1931/1937-38/1938/1946-47); systematic 4x3 tile survey of Zone A at full resolution; no structures resolvable inside Zone A except one node on Trabuco Creek

Limiting or contradictory evidence. 1946-47 frame has a coverage gap over the NW quarter of the footprint

Citation. OC Survey Historic_Imagery_v2, frames OID 346/351/310/340/286/293; research/historical_imagery/README.md

EM-003 — No cattle dip vat or corral complex has been identified anywhere inside the Ladera Ranch footprint

Confidence: Low · Status: Verified - confidence downgraded

Supporting evidence. Systematic tile survey of Zone A on the 1937-38 frame at 1.15 ft/px, a resolution at which a corral would be unmistakable; also examined 1929, 1946-47, 1948/1968/1974 topographic and orthophoto sheets

Limiting or contradictory evidence. SUBSTANTIALLY REVISED 2026-07-19, see correction C-004. USDA Circular 174 documents that California vats were frequently SMALL: a cage vat cost $55-65 in materials and 'is installed near the corrals and requires only a short chute'; for cage vats 'draining pens are not essential and are rarely used'. A wade tank cost 'less than $10 for materials' and measures roughly 15 ft long by 4 ft deep. Structures of that class need not produce a large corral complex and may not be resolvable at 1.15 ft/px. The earlier claim that 'a corral complex would be unmistakable' was overconfident. PLUS the original limits: imagery begins 1929, twelve years after the programme; vats were commonly backfilled; USGS symbology had no vat symbol.

Citation. research/historical_imagery/README.md sections 3 and 5

EM-004 — Orange County publishes scanned aerial photography of the study area back to 1929

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Raster catalog query returned 36 frames intersecting the footprint spanning 1929-1997; six pre-date 1950; frames retrieved and georeferencing verified against modern landmarks

Limiting or contradictory evidence. None

Citation. https://ocgis.com/arcpub/rest/services/Historic_Imagery/Historic_Imagery_v2/ImageServer

EM-005 — The 1968 USGS field survey mapped 41 surface-water bodies of 350 sq m or larger within the study footprint, 16 of them inside Zone A

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Cyan hydrography ink extracted from the georeferenced 1968 sheet by colour threshold; 41 features passed area and elongation filters

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Derived by colour thresholding, so inherits cartographic generalisation and scan artefacts; positions are centroids not digitised outlines; features under 350 sq m excluded by design

Citation. USGS San Juan Capistrano 7.5' quadrangle, 1968 edition; pipelines/python/extract_topo_water.py

EM-006 — Every one of the 41 ranch-era water bodies now has a modern building within 500 metres; median distance to the nearest building is 66 metres

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Measured against 10618 OSM building footprints; 24/41 within 100 m, 34/41 within 200 m, 40/41 within 300 m, 41/41 within 500 m

Limiting or contradictory evidence. OSM completeness is not guaranteed uniform, though under-mapping would bias distances upward not downward, so the finding is conservative

Citation. pipelines/python/premise_homes_on_water.py; research/historical_imagery/premise_homes_on_water.json

EM-007 — Modern housing was NOT preferentially sited on the ranch-era water bodies

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Zone-A-restricted null: water sites average 9.0 buildings within 100 m vs 9.3 for 6000 random points in the same area. Enrichment 0.97x, permutation p = 0.51

Limiting or contradictory evidence. A bbox-wide null gave 0.79x, inflated by dense Mission Viejo inside the extent; the Zone-A-restricted null is the fair comparison and is reported as primary

Citation. research/historical_imagery/premise_null_zonea.json

EM-008 — Modern slope beneath buildings cannot be used to infer original landform siting

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Buildings sit at median 1.7 degrees vs 6.7 degrees for available land, but Ladera Ranch was mass-graded, so pad flatness was manufactured by the grading under investigation. The comparison is circular.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. None - this is a methodological exclusion, deliberately recorded so the figure is not later mistaken for a finding

Citation. research/historical_imagery/README.md section 6; USGS 3DEP DEM

EM-009 — A single structure existed at 33.55505 -117.65492 on Trabuco Creek in the pre-development period

Confidence: Medium · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Visible on the 1948 USGS sheet at a trail convergence on the valley floor at elevation 307, adjacent to water; setting is consistent with a ranch working area

Limiting or contradictory evidence. The map does not label it. A single building is equally consistent with a line camp, barn, or ranch house. It is NOT identified as a dip site.

Citation. USGS San Juan Capistrano 7.5' quadrangle 1948 edition; research/historical_imagery/07_zoom_1948_ranch_structure_elev307.jpg

EM-010 — The watercourse containing the 1948 ranch structure is Trabuco Creek, not Canada Chiquita

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. The 1937-38 OC Survey aerial carries the county cartographer's own red ink label across the corridor, legible at 1.15 ft/px

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Earlier project documentation misidentified it as Canada Chiquita; corrected and logged as C-002

Citation. OC Survey Historic_Imagery_v2 frame OID 310; research/CORRECTIONS.md C-002

EM-011 — Orange County was within the California cattle tick quarantine area and was classified by the USDA as heavily tick-infested

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. USDA Bureau of Animal Industry Circular 174 (1911), p.285, lists Orange County among counties found 'heavily infested' with cattle ticks, alongside San Luis Obispo, Santa Barbara, San Diego, Fresno and Ventura. The same page refers to losses 'south of the quarantine line in California'. Page read and verified directly, not via summary. Circular 174 is titled 'Eradicating Cattle Ticks in California' and is a US Government work.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Reported at COUNTY level only. It does not place a dipping vat on any specific parcel, on Rancho Mission Viejo, or within the Ladera Ranch footprint. Orange County in 1911 covered roughly 800 square miles. This establishes the programme's geographic scope, not any site-specific activity.

Citation. MacKellar, W.M. and Hart, G.H. (1911) 'Eradicating Cattle Ticks in California', USDA Bureau of Animal Industry Circular No. 174, p.285. archive.org/details/CAT31283802

EM-013 — DTSC found arsenic in soil at several school sites on former agricultural land near Ladera Ranch

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. EnviroStor records for Carl Hankey Elementary, Plant Depot School Site, and San Juan Elementary list arsenic among contaminants found

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Arsenic occurs naturally in California soils at background levels; presence alone does not establish an anthropogenic source or a link to cattle dipping. Former orchard land also carries lead arsenate residues from a completely different practice.

Citation. DTSC EnviroStor site records

EM-019 — Cattle dipping demonstrably occurred in Orange County in 1908, at four named locations, county-ordered and supervised by public officials

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Los Angeles Herald, 27 May 1908, p.10, datelined Santa Ana: reports Dr. Coleman, deputy state quarantine officer, consulting with Orange County cattle raisers, and states that 'several hundred head of stock have been treated by dipping at the ranch of J. C. Joplin in Trabuco canyon, six or seven hundred head are now being dipped at Capistrano and dipping is in progress at Yorba and at the Bixby ranch in Santa Ana canyon.' ARTICLE READ AND VERIFIED DIRECTLY at cdnc.ucr.edu. Corroborated by Los Angeles Herald 25 July 1908 (dipping ordered by county veterinarian Dr. W.S. McFarlane; Levengood claim for six cattle deaths) and Pacific Rural Press 15 Aug 1908.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Names RANCHES AND DISTRICTS, NOT vat coordinates. None of the four named locations is Rancho Mission Viejo, and none falls inside Zone A. Measured distances from the Ladera Ranch centroid: Capistrano 3.4 mi (inside Zone B), Trabuco Canyon 8.4 mi (OUTSIDE Zone B), Yorba 25.6 mi, Santa Ana Canyon 22.8 mi. SUPERSEDED 2026-07-20: this field previously stated 'NO DIP CHEMISTRY IS NAMED for any Orange County location'. THAT IS NO LONGER TRUE - see EM-035 and EM-036. The California State Veterinarian's 1908 report names the arsenical dip, gives its formula, and describes an Orange County dipping incident using it. Do not cite the superseded wording.

Citation. Los Angeles Herald, Vol.35 No.238, 27 May 1908, p.10, 'Hope to Save Cattle from the Texas Tick'. cdnc.ucr.edu/?a=d&d=LAH19080527.2.83.22.7.3

EM-020 — The Orange County cattle tick quarantine ran from approximately February 1908 to March 1912

Confidence: Medium · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. San Francisco Call, 8 Mar 1912: 'A proclamation lifting the quarantine against the Texas fever [tick] in Orange county... was issued today by State Veterinarian Keane and signed by Governor Johnson.' Los Angeles Herald 7 Feb 1909 cites a Gillett proclamation of 1 Feb 1908. Further proclamations referenced 31 Mar 1910. Tick presence in Orange County reported as early as Nov 1898.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. REFINES AND NARROWS the '1907-1917' window this project had assumed. That figure was a general California/national approximation; the Orange-County-specific record indicates roughly 1908-1912. The proclamations themselves have NOT been read - these are newspaper reports OF proclamations, graded B2. The primary instruments are held by the California State Library and could not be retrieved (DE-010). A narrower window means a longer gap to the 1929 imagery: 17 years, not 12.

Citation. San Francisco Call, 8 Mar 1912; Los Angeles Herald, 7 Feb 1909. Via cdnc.ucr.edu

EM-022 — The Phase I environmental historical review of aerial photography for this land began in 1952

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. EIR 589 Appendix I states across all nine Planning Areas: 'Photographs dating from 1952 to 1999 were reviewed at Continental Aerial Photo' (two areas read 1953); topographic maps 1948-1988. The Oso Grande School Phase I - the one document inside the Ladera Ranch footprint - reviewed five aerials: 1952, 1968, 1977, 1989, 1994. Verified by direct text extraction across all 218 and 67 pages respectively; regex sweep for pre-1952 years returned only 1920, 1942 and 1948, all map references. Documents archived in evidence/documents/.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Describes documents OBTAINED AND READ. The Ladera Ranch entitlement EIR was NOT located, and its hazardous-materials appendix (Michael Brandman Associates, May 1995, Appendix H) is not online. This is NOT a criticism of the consultants: ASTM 1527 requires review to first developed use or 1940 using REASONABLY ASCERTAINABLE sources, and Continental Aerial Photo's holdings began in 1952. The 1929-1947 county imagery this project used is a modern digitisation that was not reasonably ascertainable in 2002.

Citation. EIR 589 Appendix I (SCH 2003021141), Phase I ESA PA 1-9, 2003 rev. 2004; Oso Grande School Phase I ESA, Feb 2002 (EnviroStor 30020004)

EM-023 — No environmental review document obtained contains any reference to cattle dipping, dip vats, or arsenical dip

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Full-text search across EIR 589 Appendix I (582,000 chars), the 2016 Ranch Plan PEIR (4.4 million chars), and the Oso Grande Phase I and Addendum. 'dip vat' 0, 'cattle dip' 0, 'sheep dip' 0. The single 'dipping' hit in EIR 589 App I (p.181) concerns metal-finishing at an industrial facility; the single 'arsenic' hit (p.119) concerns groundwater at a mine-tailings reservoir. Oso Grande: 'dip' 0, 'arsenic' 0, 'livestock' 0.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Statement about DOCUMENTS OBTAINED, not about all documents that exist. The Ladera Ranch entitlement EIR and its 1995 hazardous-materials appendix were not located. Absence from the searchable record examined here is NOT evidence that the question was never considered.

Citation. Full-text extraction, documents archived in evidence/documents/

EM-024 — The one assessed site inside the Ladera Ranch footprint was cleared with no soil sampling

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Oso Grande School, 10 acres, EnviroStor 30020004, inside the footprint at 33.5436/-117.6377. EnviroStor records past use 'AGRICULTURAL - LIVESTOCK'. No soil sampling performed. Pesticides addressed qualitatively: 'pesticides and/or herbicides are not suspected within the near-surface soils beneath the site. Any pesticides and/or herbicides at the site were likely mixed and/or placed at lower depths during the development of the site.' DTSC required a PEA 8 Sep 2003, then on 7 Oct 2003 accepted the Addendum and concluded 'further environmental investigation of the site is not required', based on a site visit and geotechnical borings reviewed for methane and organics. Final status: NO CONTAMINANTS FOUND / NO ACTION REQUIRED.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. This is a legitimate outcome under the applicable process, and a Phase I ESA is non-sampling by definition under ASTM 1527 - so absence of sampling is a property of the instrument, not a finding about the soil. 'No contaminants found' means none were looked for in soil. Tract-level grading and geotechnical reports, and any developer-commissioned sampling for the residential areas, are not in the public repositories searched and may exist.

Citation. Oso Grande School Phase I ESA (Feb 2002) and Addendum (Oct 2003); DTSC letters 8 Sep and 7 Oct 2003; DTSC EnviroStor site 30020004

EM-026 — Rancho Mission Vieja or La Paz was granted 4 April 1845 by Governor Pio Pico to Agustin Olvera, contained 46,432.65 acres, and was patented 6 August 1866

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. PRIMARY SOURCE: Ogden Hoffman, Reports of Land Cases Determined in the U.S. District Court, Northern District of California (1862), Appendix claim no.396, verbatim: 'Juan Foster, claimant for Mission Vieja or La Paz, in Los Angeles county, granted April 4th, 1845, by Pio Pico to Agustin Olvera; claim filed October 16th, 1852, confirmed by the Commission October 31st, 1854, by the District Court February 21st, 1857, and appeal dismissed June 4th, 1857; containing 46,432.65 acres.' READ AND VERIFIED DIRECTLY from full text, corroborated by the volume's own index. Acreage independently matched by Lewis (1890) p.825, Armor (1921) p.36, and the California Secretary of State US Surveyor General grant-map list, which also gives the patent date 6 Aug 1866.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. FORSTER WAS THE CLAIMANT, NOT THE GRANTEE - the grantee was Agustin Olvera; several secondary accounts collapse this. County given as Los Angeles; Orange County was not created until 1889. Acreage is the CONFIRMED figure and need not match the original Mexican grant description. A grant date of 1842 circulating online is contradicted by this record - see C-005. Whether the Ladera Ranch footprint fell within Mission Viejo, within Trabuco (a separate grant, 22,184.47 acres), or across the boundary is NOT resolved.

Citation. Hoffman, Ogden (1862), Reports of Land Cases, Appendix p.55 claim 396. archive.org/details/reportsoflandcas01hoff

EM-029 — Rancho Santa Margarita y Las Flores was a separate grant, made in 1841 by Governor Alvarado to Pio and Andres Pico, in San Diego County

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Hoffman (1862), Appendix claim no.700: 'Pio Pico et al., claimants for Santa Margarita and Las Flores, in San Diego county, granted May 10th, 1841, by Juan B. Alvarado to Pio Pico and Andres Pico; claim filed March 2d, 1853, and confirmed by the Commission April 24th, 1855.' Forster acquired it by PURCHASE from Pio Pico c.1863-64, not by grant.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Different grantees, governor, date and county from Mission Viejo. Lewis (1890) records that only 3,616 acres of the Santa Margarita holding lay inside Orange County. Do not confuse with two other California ranchos also named Santa Margarita, in Marin and San Luis Obispo counties. The 1879 patent date reported for this rancho is UNVERIFIED.

Citation. Hoffman (1862), Appendix p.95 claim 700

EM-030 — The county zoning instrument for Ladera Ranch is titled 'Ladera Planned Community', dated 17 October 1995, entitling 2,390 gross acres and 8,100 maximum dwelling units

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Ladera Planned Community Program Text, 136 pages, published by OC Public Works. Cover page RENDERED AND READ DIRECTLY: 'LADERA PLANNED COMMUNITY / PROGRAM TEXT / OCTOBER 17, 1995 / REVISED JULY 30, 2003 / PLANNED COMMUNITY STATISTICAL SUMMARY AMENDED BY ORANGE COUNTY PLANNING COMMISSION'. Statistical table gives 2,390 gross acres and 8,100 max dwelling units. Document archived at evidence/documents/.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. The adopting ORDINANCE NUMBER and Board of Supervisors action date were NOT found - the PDF is a scanned image with no text layer and no adoption page in the sections examined. Rancho Mission Viejo's own website states the plan was approved in 1997, conflicting with the 1995 cover date; a plausible reconciliation is 1995 zoning adoption vs a later development agreement, but that is INFERENCE, not established. Acreage conflicts across sources: 2,390 zoned vs ~4,000 commonly cited vs 3,165 Census CDP area.

Citation. Ladera Planned Community Program Text, 17 Oct 1995 rev. 30 Jul 2003. OC Public Works, pwds.oc.gov/sites/ocpwocds/files/import/data/files/23433.pdf

EM-032 — The western edge of the Ladera Ranch study area is sectioned PLSS land in T7S R8W; only the central and eastern portions are unsectioned grant land

Confidence: High · Status: Verified - corrects prior finding

Supporting evidence. Queried directly against the BLM CadNSDI PLSS service, section layer. West-east transect at lat 33.5467: -117.678 returns T7S R8W section 23; -117.672 and east return section 00. North-south transect at lon -117.675 returns T7S R8W sections 36, 25, 24, 24, 13, 12. Queries run independently by this investigation, not taken from a summary.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. The community CENTROID does fall in section 00, so the earlier finding was correct about that point and wrong only in being generalised to the whole footprint. The 1948 ranch structure at 33.55505/-117.65492 also falls in section 00.

Citation. BLM National PLSS CadNSDI MapServer, layer 2 (PLSS Section), queried 2026-07-20; see correction C-007

EM-033 — The survey underlying the 1866 US patent for Rancho Mission Vieja or La Paz was approved 18 December 1858

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. US Land Commission case file 250 SD, page 85 - Surveyor General's certificate of advertisement, rendered at 500 dpi and READ DIRECTLY: 'I, J. W. MANDEVILLE, United States Surveyor General, for the State of California, do hereby certify that the Rancho "Mission Viejo" or "La Paz" confirmed to Juan Forster, has been surveyed by this office, and that the survey and plat was approved by the U. S. Surveyor General, on the 18th day of December, 1858.' Records advertisement in the San Bernardino Herald (27 Sep - 16 Oct 1861) and Los Angeles Star (29 Sep - 2 Oct 1861), with a later certification of true copy dated 1866. Sequence: survey 1858, advertised 1861, certified copy 1866, patent 6 Aug 1866.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. A separate survey by J. C. Hays dated 19 December 1867 is indexed in Orange County GIS (LA Book 3/67-68). Being AFTER the 1866 patent it cannot be the survey the patent rested on. What it actually is - a resurvey, a county copy, something else - is UNRESOLVED. Some handwritten dates on the certificate are difficult to read with certainty; the 1858 survey approval date is clear, the signature date less so.

Citation. Bancroft Library, US Land Commission case file 250 SD p.85; archived at evidence/documents/

EM-035 — Arsenical dip was the standard California cattle dipping solution from 1907, and its formula is documented

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908), read directly: 'The arsenical dip was first used in California during 1907, and such good results were obtained that it has been our most effective remedy ever since.' The formula given: Arsenic 8 pounds, Carbonate of soda 24 pounds, Soap 24 pounds, Pine tar 1 gallon, Water 500 gallons. The report adds: 'This solution, being poisonous in nature, is carefully used in order that cattle dipped in same are not poisoned themselves.'

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Statewide practice, not a site-specific finding. It establishes WHAT was used in California from 1907 onward. It does not establish that any vat existed on Rancho Mission Viejo or within the Ladera Ranch footprint.

Citation. California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908), p.10; archived at evidence/documents/

EM-036 — Orange County cattle were dipped in the arsenical solution, in a swim vat, in the 1907-1908 period

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908), read directly: 'One notable case occurred in Orange County, where a number of cattle were dipped in this solution preparatory to moving them on to a tick free pasture. Subsequently several head of these cattle died from what was claimed to be arsenical poisoning, due to the cattle drinking the dip while swimming through the vat.' 'This solution' refers to the arsenical dip whose formula is given on the same page. 'SWIMMING THROUGH THE VAT' indicates a SWIM VAT - the large permanent design, not a cage vat or wade tank.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. The State Veterinarian DISPUTED the arsenical-poisoning claim: 'Upon autopsy not the slightest indication of arsenical poisoning could be found.' He attributed the deaths to exhaustion, the cattle having been 'driven a distance of some 30 to 35 miles' on the day they were dipped. This is an OFFICIAL COUNTER-NARRATIVE to the newspaper account of the Levengood claim and the two should be read together. CRITICALLY: the vat is placed in 'Orange County' with NO location given. It is not attributed to Rancho Mission Viejo and not placed within the Ladera Ranch footprint.

Citation. California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908), pp.10-11; archived at evidence/documents/

EM-037 — Cattle were driven 30 to 35 miles in a single day to reach dipping facilities in Orange County

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908): the cattle in the Orange County incident 'were driven a distance of some 30 to 35 miles' on the day they were dipped.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. A single documented instance, not a general practice statement. It CUTS BOTH WAYS for this investigation: it shows cattle could be dipped very far from their home range, which REDUCES the necessity of an on-ranch vat - but equally shows cattle from a wide catchment converged on whichever facilities existed.

Citation. California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908), p.10

EM-038 — The Orange County quarantine was not fully lifted in March 1912; a small area in the extreme southwestern part of the county remained quarantined

Confidence: High · Status: Verified - map examined; boundary indicative, not determinative

Supporting evidence. California State Veterinarian, Sixth Biennial Report (1912), read directly: 'March 7, 1912, by proclamation, your Excellency removed from quarantine all of Orange County EXCEPT A SMALL AREA IN THE EXTREME SOUTHWESTERN PART of said county. This leaves at the end of June 30, 1912, only San Diego County, that part of San Luis Obispo County lying west of...' THE MAP HAS NOW BEEN EXAMINED (p.11, rendered at 500 dpi and read directly). It shows San Diego County hatched almost entirely, and Orange County essentially clear EXCEPT a small solid wedge at its extreme southern coastal corner where it adjoins San Diego County - the San Clemente / San Onofre area.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. The residual area is NOT delimited in the proclamation text. The map is STATE-SCALE: county outlines are generalised and the drafting cannot resolve a four-square-mile community. Reading the wedge as the San Clemente/San Onofre coastal corner is an INTERPRETATION OF A SMALL-SCALE MAP, not a boundary determination. On that reading Ladera Ranch, roughly 8-10 miles north, falls OUTSIDE the residual area - but the map cannot prove it, and the underlying 7 March 1912 proclamation has NOT been obtained. Note also that the adjoining San Diego County land was Rancho Santa Margarita y Las Flores, under the same O'Neill/Flood ownership as Rancho Mission Viejo.

Citation. California State Veterinarian, Sixth Biennial Report (1912); archived at evidence/documents/

EM-039 — Orange County was one of only three counties under strict quarantine in California in 1908

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908): 'To sum up: San Diego, Imperial and Orange counties are at present the only strictly quarantined counties left in the state.'

Limiting or contradictory evidence. A statement about regulatory status at one date, at county level. It indicates the tick problem in Orange County was among the most severe in California, but places no facility anywhere.

Citation. California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908)

Chapter 26

Historical Context

Accurate general background that situates the investigation but is not itself a finding about this specific land. This distinction does most of the work in this report: that arsenical dipping existed as a practice is context. That it happened here would be a finding, and it is not one of these.

4 claims in this category.

EM-014 — Residential subdivisions carry no legal duty of soil assessment equivalent to that imposed on school sites

Confidence: Medium · Status: PENDING VERIFICATION

Supporting evidence. California Education Code requires environmental assessment before a school district acquires a site; no equivalent statutory mandate applies to residential subdivision approval

Limiting or contradictory evidence. The precise statutory citations and any applicable county or CEQA-level requirements have NOT yet been verified in this investigation. Under active research.

Citation. Requires primary verification - CA Education Code sections pending

EM-015 — USDA-specified California dipping vats included small, low-cost designs that would not necessarily produce a large or photographically distinctive surface footprint

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. USDA BAI Circular 174 (1911) p.299: cage vat materials cost $55-65 and the vat 'is installed near the corrals and requires only a short chute'; 'Draining pens with such vats are not essential and are rarely used, as the animal drains while the cage is being raised.' The wade tank 'can be constructed with an outlay of less than $10 for materials' and is 'recommended only in the disinfection of very small herds'. Figure 10 shows a vertical section approximately 15 ft long and 4 ft deep. Pages read directly.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Circular 174 also documents larger swim vats (Fig. 7, ~2,088 gallons at 5 ft depth) which WOULD leave a substantial footprint. Which design, if any, was used on any particular ranch is unknown. This describes the range of possibilities, not what existed here.

Citation. USDA BAI Circular No. 174 (1911), pp.295-299, figs 7 and 10. archive.org/details/CAT31283802

EM-016 — Arsenical dip solution was officially recognised as acutely poisonous, and USDA required a poison warning sign at every vat

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. USDA BAI Circular 174 (1911) p.293 reproduces the required vat notice, reading in part 'WARNING! The fluid in this vat is POISONOUS to man and all animals.' Circulars 183 (1911) and 207 (1912) each carry a section headed 'Precautions in the use of arsenic' addressing absorption through cuts and vapour, and warning of livestock and human fatality.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. This establishes that the practice was recognised as hazardous and was federally documented. It says nothing about whether any such vat operated on this land, nor about the fate of any residue.

Citation. USDA BAI Circulars 174 (1911) p.293, 183 (1911) p.8, and 207 (1912). archive.org

EM-017 — Photographs of Santa Margarita Ranch cattle operations circa 1900 exist, but depict San Diego County land, not the Ladera Ranch footprint

Confidence: High · Status: Verified

Supporting evidence. Four photographs acquired from USC Digital Library / California Historical Society Collection: CHS-2358, CHS-2359, CHS-2360 (all dated 1900) and CHS-2085 (dated 1886). All are public domain.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. CRITICAL INTERPRETATION BOUNDARY: the repository titles place these on the Santa Margarita ranch in SAN DIEGO COUNTY - the Rancho Santa Margarita y Las Flores property, now largely Camp Pendleton. That is NOT the Ladera Ranch footprint. The properties shared ownership under the O'Neill and Flood interests and were partitioned in the 1940s, but they are different land. These images illustrate the ranching operation generally and must NOT be presented as depicting Ladera Ranch. Also: the repository spells it 'Marguerita' for CHS-2085, and dates it 1886, not the 1887 commonly cited online.

Citation. USC Libraries Special Collections, California Historical Society Collection 1860-1960; DOIs 10.25549/chs-m9636, m9635, m9638, m7221

Chapter 27

Investigative Leads

Specific, testable propositions supported by partial evidence, each carrying a defined next step. A lead is a question with a method attached. None of these is a finding, and none should be cited as one.

3 claims in this category.

EM-027 — Ownership of the Forster ranch holdings passed successively to Charles Crocker, then James Flood, then Richard O'Neill

Confidence: Low · Status: Downgraded from ESTABLISHED FACT per C-006

Supporting evidence. Armor (1921): 'in the forties John Forster, having captured the heart of Don Pico's sister, secured the ranches also... on his death left such a heritage that... his heirs assumed indebtedness to keep the property; and when much of it was heavily mortgaged, it passed into the hands first of Charles Crocker, then of James Flood, and finally of Richard O'Neill.' Passage read and verified directly.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. SEE CORRECTION C-006. Armor contains substantive errors in this very passage: he calls the Mexican grants 'Spanish', states Mission Viejo 'originally belonged to the Picos' (Hoffman gives Agustin Olvera), and elsewhere conflates San Juan Cajon de Santa Ana with Canon de Santa Ana. THE CHARLES CROCKER LINK IS UNCORROBORATED and appears in no other source consulted. Secondary accounts of the O'Neill/Flood transaction conflict on year (1882 vs 1884), price ($457,000 vs ~$460,000), structure (equal ownership vs Flood-owned), and the half-interest date (1906 vs 1907). All are grade B2 or C. The primary record is a deed, which - Orange County not existing until 1889 - would sit in Los Angeles and San Diego County records.

Citation. Armor (1921), biographical sketch of John O. Forster

EM-031 — The Ladera Ranch entitlement records are filed under 'Ladera', not 'Ladera Ranch', which likely explains why searches for the entitlement EIR have returned nothing

Confidence: Medium · Status: Open - retest inconclusive; needs a records request, not a title search

Supporting evidence. The county's adopted zoning instrument is titled 'LADERA PLANNED COMMUNITY' - verified from the document cover. Every CEQAnet and county-repository search conducted in this investigation used the string 'Ladera Ranch' and returned zero entitlement records, including a complete half-year sweep of Orange County filings 1990-2000.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. RETEST ATTEMPTED AND INCONCLUSIVE (DE-012). Re-running the search with 'Ladera' alone against CEQAnet's serp endpoint returned zero - but a CONTROL QUERY for 'Irvine', a term certain to appear in thousands of Orange County records, ALSO returned zero. That endpoint is a site-content search, not the record search, and cannot answer the question either way. The structured record search has no free-text title field at all, which is itself the likeliest reason title-based searching has failed throughout. The hypothesis is UNTESTED, not refuted. Probing adjacent document IDs in the county store also failed - all return HTML error pages (DE-013).

Citation. Ladera Planned Community Program Text cover [S-LADERA-PC]; CEQAnet negative sweep, this investigation

EM-034 — The complete US Land Commission case files for Mission Vieja/La Paz, Trabuco, and Niguel have been obtained but not yet read

Confidence: High · Status: Open - obtained, requires palaeographic reading

Supporting evidence. Three PDFs downloaded and verified as real documents: 250 SD Mission Vieja/La Paz (96pp, 98 MB), 216 SD Trabuco (84pp, 75 MB), 130 SD Niguel (102pp, 87 MB). Land Commission files contain SWORN WITNESS TESTIMONY, which is where descriptions of ranch improvements - buildings, corrals, springs, fences - are recorded.
