Limiting or contradictory evidence. THE TESTIMONY IS HANDWRITTEN CURSIVE, partly in Spanish, and the OCR layer is useless on it. A keyword scan for 'corral', 'spring' and 'adobe' returned zero hits, which reflects OCR FAILURE, NOT ABSENCE of those terms. Extracting anything requires page-by-page palaeographic reading, which has NOT been done. No claim is made here about what these files contain.

Citation. Bancroft Library via UC Berkeley digicoll; archived at evidence/documents/

Chapter 28

Open Questions

Material questions this investigation has identified and cannot currently answer. These are stated plainly rather than smoothed over, because the shape of what is unknown is itself a result — and because a reader deserves to know where the floor gives way.

5 claims in this category.

EM-012 — Arsenical cattle dipping actually occurred within the Ladera Ranch footprint

Confidence: None · Status: OPEN

Supporting evidence. No direct evidence. Terrain logic places working facilities near water and trail convergences, and one such node exists.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. No vat found on adequate imagery. No documentary record located. No soil sampling has tested for it in the residential footprint. This is an UNPROVEN HYPOTHESIS and must not be presented as more.

Citation. No supporting citation exists - this is the central open question of the investigation

EM-018 — No 1912 plat map of Rancho Santa Margarita y Las Flores has been located; the available survey plat is dated 1867

Confidence: Medium · Status: Open

Supporting evidence. Searched Library of Congress maps and JSON API, David Rumsey, Calisphere, Online Archive of California, and archive.org. Only the 1867 Strobel survey plat surfaced (Bancroft Library, Land Case Map E-1362A, 89,742 acres). Acquired.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. BLM GLO Records could NOT be searched - the current site requires Login.gov authentication and no account was created. GLO remains the most likely repository for an early-20th-century plat. Absence here reflects an unsearched archive, NOT a demonstrated absence. Structurally, 1912 postdates the 1879 patent, so any such document would more likely be a partition or subdivision map than a Surveyor General plat.

Citation. Bancroft Library Land Case Map E-1362A, ark:/13030/hb0199n5n8; negative searches logged in DEAD_ENDS.md

EM-021 — No Orange County newspaper covering the 1908-1912 quarantine period has been digitised in any searched database

Confidence: High · Status: Open

Supporting evidence. CDNC holds only four Orange County titles and none covers 1900-1909. Chronicling America holds ZERO Orange County California titles, verified against a control (15 titles for Los Angeles County, 1 for San Diego). Every Orange County finding in this investigation came from OUT-OF-COUNTY papers reporting inward - the Los Angeles Herald and Pacific Rural Press.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. This is a DIGITISATION GAP, NOT AN ABSENCE OF RECORDS. The Santa Ana Blade and Anaheim Gazette are confirmed by name in a 1903 Pacific Rural Press item as the papers of record; neither is digitised anywhere searched. Quarantine proclamations would have been published as paid legal notices in exactly those titles. Their absence online says nothing about whether they exist on microfilm.

Citation. Coverage audit of CDNC and Chronicling America holdings, this investigation; see DEAD_ENDS.md DE-011

EM-025 — The hazardous-materials assessment for the land Ladera Ranch was built on has been identified by name but is not available online

Confidence: High · Status: Open - TOP PRIORITY RECORDS REQUEST

Supporting evidence. EIR 589 Appendix I, p.21, cites: 'Michael Brandman Associates, May 1995, Environmental Impact Report for the Antonio Parkway Roadway Alignment and Land Use Plan, Appendix H - Hazardous Materials Environmental Site Assessment.' A complete half-year CEQAnet sweep (22 queries, lead agency Orange County, 1990-2000; plus 14 by county 1993-1999) returned zero Ladera Ranch records. CEQAnet hosts no attachments for filings of that vintage.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. The identification of SCH 1994031075 (Antonio Parkway Alignment Study) as the Ladera Ranch entitlement vehicle is INFERRED from geography and a single citation, and is UNCONFIRMED. The document may or may not address cattle-era land use; its contents are unknown.

Citation. EIR 589 Appendix I, p.21; CEQAnet SCH 1994031075. Records request: Orange County Planning, (714) 834-2166

EM-028 — The terms and dates of the Forster-to-O'Neill/Flood transfers remain unverified

Confidence: High · Status: Open

Supporting evidence. RESOLVED SINCE FIRST ENTRY: the grant date (4 Apr 1845), grantee (Agustin Olvera) and patent date (6 Aug 1866) are now established from Hoffman (1862) and the California Secretary of State. What remains open is the later transfer chain.

Limiting or contradictory evidence. Secondary accounts conflict on every material detail and none has been checked against a deed. US Land Commission case files - which frequently contain survey plats and testimony describing IMPROVEMENTS AND STRUCTURES, precisely what this investigation needs - are held by the National Archives and Bancroft and have NOT been obtained. BLM GLO records remain blocked behind Login.gov (DE-007).

Citation. Gap identified from Armor (1921); primary sources not obtained

Chapter 29

Recommended Archival Work

Ordered by expected value per unit of effort. Each entry states what it would resolve and what a negative result would mean — a recommendation that cannot fail is not a test.

Archival work comes before ground work. That sequencing is itself a finding: this investigation spent considerable effort on imagery analysis, producing a negative result of limited weight, before consulting a free 1911 government circular that established whether the activity was even plausible in this county. The circular took minutes to obtain.

29.1 Top priority — the missing hazmat assessment

Michael Brandman Associates, May 1995 — "Appendix H, Hazardous Materials Environmental Site Assessment", for the Antonio Parkway Roadway Alignment and Land Use Plan.

Resolves: how far back the historical review reached for the residential footprint itself — the question Ch. 17 can only answer for neighbouring land.

Route: Orange County Planning, (714) 834-2166. Public Records Act request. CEQAnet hosts no attachments for filings of that vintage.

If negative: if it reviewed pre-1952 imagery and found nothing, the audit finding substantially weakens and the hypothesis loses much of its remaining force. That is a valuable outcome.

29.2 Orange County Board of Supervisors minutes, 1907–1912

Resolves: dipping in Orange County was county-ordered, so there must be board action, appropriations, and an inspector appointment. The Pacific Rural Press of 5 March 1910 records the state veterinarian formally petitioning the board for a second tick inspector — so the board was engaged with this in a documented way.

The E. J. Levengood claim (~$400, six cattle died, mid-1908) is dated and specific enough to locate, and a claim file would name the ranch, the location, and possibly the preparation used.

Route: Orange County Archives, Santa Ana. Not digitised anywhere searched.

29.3 Anaheim Gazette and Santa Ana Blade, on microfilm

Resolves: quarantine proclamations were published as paid legal notices in the papers of record. A Pacific Rural Press item of 1903 names both titles as exactly that.

Neither is digitised anywhere. Every Orange County finding in this investigation came from out-of-county papers reporting inward — the Los Angeles Herald and Pacific Rural Press — because CDNC holds only four OC titles and none covers 1900–1909 (DE-011).

Route: microfilm at Orange County Archives, Santa Ana Public Library, or CSU Fullerton. Target windows: Feb 1908, May–Aug 1908, Mar–Apr 1910, Mar 1912.

29.4 BLM General Land Office survey field notes

Resolves: surveyors recorded springs, corrals, houses, fences and improvements in their running notes that never reached the published plat.

Correction C-007 opened this up: the western edge of the study area is sectioned land — T7S R8W sections 12, 13, 24, 25 — not the unsectioned grant land this project had assumed throughout. Ordinary section-line field notes should exist for it.

Route: glorecords.blm.gov/s/ — no login required, contrary to this project's earlier entry (DE-007, revised). The site loads logged out and returns thousands of results. The obstacle is a scripting one, not an access one. Filter by State CA, Meridian San Bernardino, Township 7S, Range 7W and 8W, and check for a separate Field Notes record attached to each survey — it is historically a distinct document and easy to miss.

29.5 The Bancroft witness testimony — already on disk

Three complete US Land Commission case files, 272 MB, in evidence/documents/: 250 SD (Mission Vieja/La Paz, 96pp), 216 SD (Trabuco, 84pp), 130 SD (Niguel, 102pp).

Resolves: land claim proceedings turned on occupation and improvement, so sworn testimony routinely describes buildings, corrals, springs and fences.

Why it has not been done: the testimony is handwritten cursive, partly in Spanish. The OCR layer is unusable — a keyword scan for corral, spring and adobe returned zero hits, which is OCR failure, not absence.

Route: palaeographic reading. This needs a person who reads mid-nineteenth-century Spanish and English cursive, not more searching. It is the cheapest lead here in dollar terms and the most expensive in skill.

29.6 Rancho Mission Viejo's own operating records

Resolves: dipping was a mandated activity generating inspection records, chemical purchases, and correspondence with state authorities. This is the single place a facility on this ranch would necessarily have been documented.

Route: private corporate archive. No public access route. Requires a direct approach.

29.7 The 1907 Governor's proclamation

California State Library, executive-order collection, 5910-5913.pdf. Returns HTTP 403 to automated access — a bot filter, not a paywall.

An ordinary browser would probably open it in under a minute. It would date the programme's California commencement precisely and define its boundaries in the state's own terms.

29.8 Also worth pursuing

Target Holds
Sherman Library & Gardens, Corona del Mar Major Southern California ranch history collection
First American Title photograph archive, Santa Ana Extensive Orange County historical imagery
UC Irvine and CSU Fullerton Special Collections Orange County planning and history
NARA Record Group 17 BAI field records — in other states these name individual vats
California State Veterinarian biennial reports, 1908 & 1910 May enumerate vat sites by county
OC Survey, (714) 967-0852 Township plats and "Township Plats Notes", counter access only
Investigative Lead

The time-limited one is not on this list. Every archival lead above will still be available in ten years. Oral history will not — see Ch. 13. If effort has to be prioritised, living memory outranks every document here.

Chapter 30

Recommended Archival and Environmental Work

Recommendations, ordered by what would most reduce uncertainty per unit of effort. Each carries what it would resolve, and — importantly — what a negative result would mean, because a recommendation that cannot fail is not a test.

30.1 The decisive question, and it is cheap

Open Question

Was Orange County ever within the California cattle tick quarantine and eradication area?

This is the first link in the hypothesis chain. If the answer is no, the entire arsenical-dipping hypothesis closes, and this investigation will have produced a clean negative result rather than an open question.

It should be answerable from documents, at low cost, without anyone touching soil.

Where to look, in order:

  1. USDA Bureau of Animal Industry annual reports. Published annually through the eradication era, they enumerate quarantine areas by state and county and record acreage released from quarantine. Full-text searchable on the Internet Archive, HathiTrust, and Google Books.
  2. California State Veterinarian biennial reports and California State Board of Agriculture reports. Same period, state-level detail.
  3. California Department of Agriculture monthly bulletins.
  4. National Archives Record Group 17 (Bureau of Animal Industry) field records. Mostly not digitised; reproduction request required.
  5. Legal notices in period newspapers. Quarantine proclamations and dipping orders were published as legal notices. The California Digital Newspaper Collection at UC Riverside is free and full-text searchable, as is Chronicling America.

What a negative result means: if the programme demonstrably did not extend to Orange County, the hypothesis is closed and should be publicly retired. That is a real and valuable outcome, and this project commits to publishing it as prominently as a positive one.

30.2 Rancho Mission Viejo's own records

The ranch operated continuously across the relevant period. Dipping was a mandated activity that generated paperwork: inspection records, chemical purchases, correspondence with state authorities, and operational maps.

This is now the highest-value archival lead in the investigation, because the imagery avenue is close to exhausted and the ranch's internal record is the one place a facility would necessarily have been documented if it existed.

It is a private corporate archive with no public access route. It requires a direct approach.

30.3 General Land Office survey field notes

Investigative Lead

The highest-value untapped cartographic source identified by this investigation.

Surveyors recorded, in their field notes, features that never reached the published plat: springs, corrals, houses, fences, roads, and "improvements" encountered on the section lines they walked. The published plat is a summary; the notes are the observation.

Free and available at the BLM GLO Records site for the townships covering this land.

Because Ladera Ranch sits on unsectioned land-grant land, the relevant notes may be those of the surrounding townships and the grant boundary survey rather than interior section lines. That narrows the search but does not close it.

30.4 Regional archival collections

None of these has been exhausted, and several have not been searched at all.

Holding Relevance Access
Sherman Library & Gardens, Corona del Mar Major Southern California ranch history collection In-person appointment
First American Title historical photograph archive Extensive Orange County imagery In-person / reproduction
Orange County Archives Original plats, assessor records, county correspondence In-person
UC Irvine Special Collections Orange County history Finding aids online
Huntington Library Southern California ranch and land-grant materials Reader registration
Mission San Juan Capistrano archives Mission-period and early rancho land records Request
Bancroft Library, UC Berkeley Spanish and Mexican land grant records Reader registration

Photograph collections deserve particular emphasis. A single photograph of ranch cattle operations showing a vat, corral, or chute would materially change the evidentiary picture — in either direction. Ranch photography from this period exists; it has not been systematically searched.

30.5 Oral history — and why it is time-limited

Investigative Lead

Ranch hands, foremen, veterinarians, and family members who could describe mid-century operations directly are now very elderly or deceased. Every other line of inquiry in this document will still be available in ten years. This one will not.

If oral history is attempted, the questions should be open rather than leading. Asking "was there a dipping vat?" invites confabulation, particularly from people trying to be helpful about a subject they know matters. Asking "where did you work the cattle, and what was there?" produces testimony rather than agreement. Any account obtained should be recorded as unverified testimony and corroborated before it carries weight.

30.6 Environmental review documents

Obtain and read in full, rather than inferring their contents from their absence online:

  • The certified EIR for the Ladera Ranch planned community, including technical appendices
  • The Rancho Mission Viejo Ranch Plan EIR
  • Any Phase I and Phase II Environmental Site Assessments
  • Grading plans with cut-and-fill volumes and soil import sources

The specific audit question is narrower than "was the site studied." It is:

  1. How far back did the Phase I historical review actually go? Standard practice reviews aerial photography at roughly ten-year intervals — but from what starting date?
  2. Was sampling ever targeted at point-source locations, as opposed to gridded at field scale for broad agricultural residue? These are different investigations that sound identical in summary.
  3. Do the review materials contain the terms "dipping", "vat", or "cattle dip" anywhere?
Limitation

Wording discipline on this point is mandatory. Until these documents are obtained and read, the correct statement is "the searchable materials examined in this investigation do not appear to identify…" — never "they never studied…". A document sitting in a county file cabinet is invisible to this project and must not be declared nonexistent by it.

30.7 Physical investigation, if the archival work supports it

Geophysics first. Ground-penetrating radar or electromagnetic survey at the Trabuco ranch node and a prioritised subset of the 41 mapped water bodies. This detects buried concrete and disturbed fill directly, is non-destructive, and is far cheaper than sampling at the same spatial density.

Then targeted soil sampling, with the right analytes.

The single most important methodological point in this entire document:

Investigative Lead

Test arsenic and lead together, and establish local background first.

  • Orchard lead arsenate produces elevated lead and arsenic in roughly compound proportion, across a broad area — because it was sprayed across whole fields.
  • A cattle dipping vat produces sharply elevated arsenic in a very small area, without proportionate lead.

These signatures are distinguishable. Testing arsenic alone cannot separate them, and would produce a result that supports either explanation equally — which is to say, no result at all.

Without a local background determination, no measurement can be interpreted, because California soils carry naturally occurring arsenic and the background is the comparison every finding depends on.

A sampling grid designed for field-scale agricultural residue will not find a dip vat. A vat footprint is perhaps twenty metres across. A grid sized to characterise a field can pass directly over it and register nothing. If sampling is undertaken, it must be targeted at candidate point-source locations — which is precisely what the sampling frame produced by this investigation provides.

30.8 The pathway that deserves more attention than it has received

Investigative Lead

Obtain well-integrity and plugging records, and any soil-gas or methane monitoring data, for the two plugged exploratory wells within approximately one mile of the community centroid.

This is the only pathway examined in this investigation with a published epidemiological association with Ewing sarcoma specifically — suggestive and statistically non-significant, but more direct than anything supporting the hypothesis that prompted the work.

It has received a fraction of the analytical effort. That imbalance reflects where the project started, not where the evidence points.

30.9 What this project will not do

Stated so the boundaries are explicit and testable by anyone reading:

  • It will not collect, request, or store individual health information.
  • It will not identify or attempt to infer individual cases.
  • It will not enter private property or sample soil without the owner's consent and appropriate professional oversight.
  • It will not present any finding as establishing causation.
  • It will not retire a hypothesis quietly. If the dipping hypothesis is closed by evidence, that will be published as prominently as any positive finding.
Chapter 31

Limitations

A limitations section that lists only minor caveats is a marketing document. This one is intended to be usable by someone trying to determine how much weight this publication can bear.

Several of the limitations below are severe enough that they should change how a reader reads everything preceding them.

31.1 Structural limitations — these do not improve with more work

This is not an epidemiological study. No individual health data were collected, requested, or analysed. Whether the reported cases constitute a statistical excess is not determined here, and no analysis in this publication is capable of determining it. Any reader looking for that answer will not find it here, and should not infer it from anything that is here.

Spatial coincidence is not exposure. Every geographic finding establishes proximity and nothing else. It does not establish that a substance was present, that it persisted, that anyone contacted it, or in what quantity. The chain from proximity to exposure to dose to harm has three unbridged links, and this publication crosses none of them.

Ecological inference cannot transfer to individuals. Community-scale patterns say nothing about any individual. This is a permanent property of the data, not a temporary gap.

Absence of online records is not absence of records. This investigation is conducted almost entirely from publicly reachable sources. Documents held in county file cabinets, private corporate archives, or un-digitised collections are invisible to it. Every statement in this publication about what "does not appear" in the record is a statement about what this investigation could reach, not about what exists.

31.2 Evidentiary limitations

The imagery post-dates the period of interest. The earliest photography is 1929; compulsory dipping ran to about 1917. Twelve years is a substantial improvement on the thirty-one this project previously believed, but it does not close the gap. A facility decommissioned and backfilled in 1917 could be entirely invisible by 1929. The negative imagery finding is therefore bounded: it is meaningful evidence against a surviving surface facility, and no evidence at all about a demolished one.

The central premise is unverified. Whether California's tick eradication programme applied to Orange County at all has not been confirmed against primary sources. Two claims in the evidence matrix (EM-011, EM-014) are explicitly flagged PENDING VERIFICATION for this reason. A reader should treat the entire dipping hypothesis as resting on an unconfirmed foundation until that flag is cleared.

No soil measurement exists for the residential footprint. Every statement about arsenic in this study area derives from sampling at school sites, which are different parcels with different histories. Nothing in this publication reports a measurement from Ladera Ranch soil, because none was located.

Named ranch facilities remain unlocated. Headquarters, cow camps, line camps, and named corrals are referenced in the historical record but none has been located with coordinates by this investigation. Whether any stood within the study footprint — as opposed to elsewhere on a ranch covering well over a hundred thousand acres — is unresolved.

31.3 Data quality limitations

Pesticide use reporting cannot resolve to this community. 94.6% of 79,473 Orange County records could not be located more precisely than a large grid cell. Within landscape maintenance specifically, 22 of 15,383 records (0.1%) carried usable coordinates. The landscape-pesticide pathway is not weakly supported — it is unmeasurable with the available instrument, which is a different and more honest statement.

Building footprints are community-mapped. OpenStreetMap completeness is not guaranteed uniform. For the water-proximity analysis this biases measured distances upward, making that result conservative — but the same caveat would not necessarily be favourable for a different analysis.

The water layer is derived, not digitised from survey data. It was extracted by colour threshold from a scanned printed sheet. It inherits cartographic generalisation and scan artefacts. Positions are centroids, not outlines. Features under 350 m² were excluded by design and are absent regardless of whether they existed.

Study zones are approximate. Zone A is a bounding box, not a cadastral boundary. Any analysis sensitive to its exact edges should not use it.

31.4 Methodological failures recorded

Recorded because a limitations section that omits the methods that failed is not complete.

Automated water detection failed and was abandoned. A detector keyed on brightness and texture returned roughly 100 candidates inside Zone A and was found to be detecting hillslope shadow. None of its output appears in this publication.

Automated structure detection was 4% accurate. A blob detector on the 1948 sheet returned 25 candidates; 24 were map-label letters, section-line dots, and a benchmark mark. Only visual verification prevented them from being published as findings.

A terrain analysis was invalidated by its own subject. Buildings sit at median 1.7° slope against 6.7° for available land — a striking figure that cannot be used, because mass grading manufactured the flatness being measured.

A derived drainage network was too sparse to use. The flow-accumulation threshold was set too conservatively, producing a channel network inconsistent with the visible drainage pattern. The valley-bottom enrichment figure computed from it is not reported anywhere as a finding.

31.5 Limitations of the author and method

No formal training. This investigation was conducted by a community member without training in epidemiology, toxicology, geology, or archival research, using AI tools to organise and analyse public records. Domain experts will find things here that a specialist would have caught sooner, framed better, or avoided entirely.

AI-assisted analysis carries characteristic failure modes. These include fluent presentation of uncertain conclusions, plausible-sounding detail that is not grounded in a source, and confident restatement of an earlier error. The project's countermeasures — mandatory counter-evidence on every claim, a published dead-end log, source grading with no silent promotion, and open corrections — are mitigations, not guarantees. Readers should verify claims that matter to them against the cited sources rather than relying on this document.

Confirmation bias is the standing risk. This investigation began with a hypothesis. Two significant results have already cut against it: the corrected imagery premise, and the failed water-proximity hypothesis. Both were retained. That is the intended behaviour, and it is not evidence that the bias has been eliminated — only that it was caught twice.

31.6 What would change the conclusions

Stated so the publication is falsifiable rather than merely cautious:

Finding Effect
Documentation that Orange County was outside the tick quarantine area Closes the hypothesis entirely
A period document, photograph, or account placing a vat on this ranch Converts the central lead into a finding
Soil arsenic sharply elevated in a small area without proportionate lead Strong support for a point source
Soil arsenic elevated broadly with proportionate lead Points to orchard lead arsenate, not dipping
Geophysics finding buried concrete at the Trabuco node Materially raises priority for sampling
A Phase I ESA showing the historical review reached the 1920s and found nothing Substantially reduces the open question
Local background arsenic at or above measured concentrations Removes the anthropogenic inference

31.7 The honest summary

This publication reconstructs what the reachable public record shows about the history of this land, and identifies where that record has gaps.

It does not establish that anything harmful is present in Ladera Ranch soil. It does not establish that anyone was exposed to anything. It does not explain the reported cases, and it does not attempt to.

Its most defensible product is a map of a specific uncertainty — a bounded set of locations and a set of documentary questions, each with a method attached and a defined outcome in both directions.

That is less than the investigation set out to find. It is what the evidence supports.

Chapter 32

Conclusion

This investigation set out to answer one question: has every reasonable historical question about this land already been considered?

It can now answer that, and the answer is narrower and more useful than either the affirmative or the accusation.

32.1 What was established

The land was open cattle rangeland until development. Confirmed across six aerial photographs from 1929 to 1947, four USGS topographic editions, and the land-grant labelling on the sheets themselves.

Orange County was in the cattle tick eradication programme. USDA Bureau of Animal Industry Circular 174 (1911), page 285, lists it among counties "heavily infested," south of the California quarantine line. Read directly from the primary source.

Dipping actually happened in this county, under public order. The Los Angeles Herald of 27 May 1908 records cattle being dipped at four named Orange County locations. Six to seven hundred head at Capistrano alone. A companion article records a claim against the county after six cows died following a dip supervised by the county veterinarian.

The quarantine ran from approximately February 1908 to March 1912.

The environmental review of this land began in 1952. Forty years after the dipping era ended, and twenty-three years after the earliest aerial photograph that exists.

No document obtained contains the terms "dip vat," "cattle dip," or "arsenical dip."

The one assessed site inside the community was cleared with no soil sampling.

32.2 What was not established

Nothing places a dipping vat on this land. No vat, no corral, no map label, no photograph, no purchase record, no employee account, no soil measurement. The four locations named in 1908 do not include Rancho Mission Viejo, and the nearest — Capistrano at 3.4 miles — is outside the community.

No dip chemistry is named for any Orange County location in any source located. The cattle deaths are consistent with an arsenical dip. The sources do not say so.

Nothing in this publication bears on the reported illnesses. Not one finding here establishes that any substance was present in Ladera Ranch soil, that anyone was exposed to anything, or that any party caused any harm. That was never within the reach of a records investigation, and it is not claimed.

32.3 The hypothesis, six links later

# Proposition Status
1 The programme applied to Orange County Established
1a Dipping occurred in the county, at named sites Established
2 This ranch dipped cattle at its own facility Unevidenced
3 The facility stood within or near the footprint Unevidenced
4 Arsenic residue persists there Unevidenced
5 Development disturbed or built over it Unevidenced
6 A pathway to human exposure exists Unevidenced

One link of six, plus a partial second. The gap between "the programme reached this county" and "a vat stood here" remains completely unbridged.

The honest position is unchanged from where this publication began, though it now rests on far more evidence: the arsenical-dipping hypothesis is coherent, testable, and unsupported by any site-specific evidence. It should be neither asserted nor abandoned.

32.4 What this investigation actually found

Not a contaminant. A gap in a historical window, described precisely enough to be closed.

The environmental assessment looked back to 1952 using the aerial archive available in 2002. The activity of interest ended in 1912. The imagery that would have covered it — 1929, 1931, 1937–38, 1946–47 — exists, is free, is public, and is a modern digitisation that was not reasonably ascertainable when the assessment was done.

That is a reason to look again. It is not a finding that anyone failed. The consultants complied with the applicable standard using the sources they had. This publication says so plainly, in the audit chapter and here, because the distinction between a gap and a failure is the difference between a document that can be acted on and one that will be dismissed.

32.5 What the evidence actually points at

A finding this investigation did not expect and did not go looking for.

The hypothesis that prompted all of this ranks last among the pathways examined, on documentation. Meanwhile two plugged exploratory wells sit within about a mile of the community centroid — one at roughly a quarter-mile — and that is the only pathway with any published epidemiological association with Ewing sarcoma specifically. Suggestive, statistically non-significant, and more direct than anything supporting cattle dipping.

It surfaced from correcting an error in this project's own geometry. It has received a fraction of the analytical effort.

An investigation that reordered that table to protect its founding premise would forfeit any claim to being taken seriously. It is stated here, and in the executive summary, at the same volume as everything else.

32.6 Four corrections, one pattern

Four corrections were issued and published in full. Three share a shape:

An assumption about the physical evidence was adopted without checking it, and the assumption happened to make the analysis simpler.

  • A centroid inherited from a brief, 1.93 miles wrong (C-001)
  • The earliest imagery assumed to be 1948, making a vat undetectable in principle (C-003)
  • Having found better imagery, the target assumed to be large, making the negative result strong (C-004)

Every one was caught by going to a primary source or by rendering the data instead of trusting a table. Two of them weakened conclusions this project had already published.

The countermeasures — mandatory counter-evidence on every claim, a published dead-end log, source grading with no silent promotion, corrections issued in full — are not decoration. They are the reason this document can be checked. They are also, demonstrably, insufficient on their own: each correction was caught late, and there is no reason to believe a fifth is not sitting in these pages right now.

32.7 What should happen next

In an archive, not in the ground. In order:

  1. The Michael Brandman Associates May 1995 Appendix H hazardous-materials ESA — named in EIR 589, not online. It would answer, for the residential footprint itself, how far back the review looked. Orange County Planning, (714) 834-2166.
  2. Orange County Board of Supervisors minutes, 1907–1912. County-ordered dipping means board action, appropriations, and an inspector appointment. The Levengood claim is dated and specific enough to locate.
  3. Anaheim Gazette and Santa Ana Blade on microfilm — the papers of record, digitised nowhere. Target windows: Feb 1908, May–Aug 1908, Mar–Apr 1910, Mar 1912.
  4. Rancho Mission Viejo's own operating records — private, and the single place a facility would necessarily have been documented.
  5. BLM GLO survey field notes — where surveyors recorded springs, corrals and improvements that never reached the published plat. Requires a Login.gov account.
  6. The 1907 Governor's proclamation at the California State Library — blocked to automated access by a bot filter, and probably retrievable in an ordinary browser in under a minute.

Then, if the archival work supports it: geophysics at candidate locations, and soil sampling that tests arsenic and lead together — the ratio distinguishes an orchard lead-arsenate signature (broad, both elevated proportionally) from a dipping-vat signature (tiny, arsenic without proportionate lead). Local background must be established first, or no result can be interpreted.

32.8 The bottom line

The reported pattern warrants investigation. The available evidence does not establish causation, and this publication does not attempt to.

What it offers is a sampling frame and a records request: forty-one mapped water bodies, one ranch structure, a named document sitting in a county office, and a forty-year window that nobody has yet looked into with the imagery that now exists.

That is less than the investigation set out to find. It is what the evidence supports, and it is enough to act on.

Limitation

Every finding in this publication is checkable. Sources are cited with URLs and identifiers; the data files, scripts, and archived documents are in the repository; the corrections and dead ends are logged in full.

If any part of this is wrong, it should be corrected in the open — as four things already have been.

Chapter 34

Image Credits and Rights

Every image published, with its repository, rights status as the repository states it, and its interpretation boundary. Rights statements are reproduced rather than summarised, because a paraphrased licence is not a licence.

26 published figures.

Images held in the evidence archive but not published for rights reasons are listed at the end.

FIG-01 — The footprint across four epochs, with the 1968 water layer overprinted
Date 1929-2022
Repository This investigation
Rights Derived figure. Underlying imagery credited in the caption; derived layout CC BY 4.0.
Original 2330x2124 — research/historical_imagery/11_timeseries_1929-2022_with_water.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-01.jpg (1800x1641)

Interpretation boundary. Panels share one extent and georeferencing. Cyan circles mark water bodies as drawn by the 1968 USGS survey; they are centroids, not digitised outlines. The cyan box is an approximate screening boundary, not a legal boundary.

FIG-02 — 1929 aerial photograph, South County Watersheds frame
Date 1929
Repository OC Survey Geospatial Services, County of Orange
Rights Public record, County of Orange. Retrieved from the county's public ArcGIS image service.
Original 6000x5200 — research/historical_imagery/oc_aerials/ann_1929.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-02.jpg (1800x1560)

Interpretation boundary. Twelve years after compulsory cattle dipping ended. Open rangeland; no structures resolvable inside Zone A. The dark wedge at upper left is outside the frame's coverage, not terrain.

FIG-03 — 1937-38 aerial photograph, 600-scale county series
Date 1937-1938
Repository OC Survey Geospatial Services, County of Orange
Rights Public record, County of Orange. Retrieved from the county's public ArcGIS image service.
Original 6000x5200 — research/historical_imagery/oc_aerials/ann_1937.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-03.jpg (1800x1560)

Interpretation boundary. The sharpest frame available, at approximately 1.15 ft/px. Individual oaks, fence lines and wheel ruts are legible. No corral, pen or vat complex is resolvable inside Zone A.

FIG-04 — The same ground, built out
Date 2022
Repository OC Survey Geospatial Services, County of Orange
Rights Public record, County of Orange. Retrieved from the county's public ArcGIS image service.
Original 4000x3467 — research/historical_imagery/oc_aerials/ann_2022.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-04.jpg (1800x1560)

Interpretation boundary. Rendered to the identical extent as the historical frames. That Zone A lands on the real subdivisions is the independent check on georeferencing of the whole series.

FIG-05 — The Trabuco Creek ranch node at 100 m scale, 1937-38
Date 1937-1938
Repository OC Survey Geospatial Services, County of Orange
Rights Public record, County of Orange. Retrieved from the county's public ArcGIS image service.
Original 2730x2274 — research/historical_imagery/oc_aerials/z1_ranch_1937.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-05.jpg (1800x1499)

Interpretation boundary. Centred on 33.55505, -117.65492. The red ink label is the county cartographer's own and reads Trabuco Creek. A single structure at a trail convergence beside water is consistent with a ranch working area AND equally consistent with a line camp, barn or ranch house. It is NOT identified as a dip site.

FIG-06 — The same location today
Date 2022
Repository OC Survey Geospatial Services, County of Orange
Rights Public record, County of Orange. Retrieved from the county's public ArcGIS image service.
Original 1818x1518 — research/historical_imagery/oc_aerials/z1_ranch_2022_modern.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-06.jpg (1800x1503)

Interpretation boundary. For comparison with FIG-05 at identical extent and scale.

FIG-07 — USGS 7.5-minute San Juan Capistrano quadrangle, 1968 revision
Date 1968
Repository U.S. Geological Survey
Rights Public domain (U.S. Government work).
Original 2336x2400 — research/historical_imagery/03_topo_1968_footprint.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-07.jpg (1752x1800)

Interpretation boundary. The source of the 41-body surface-water layer. Shows the impoundment chain down the Trabuco corridor, a labelled Water Tank, a Terminal Reservoir, a landing strip and a gaging station. The land grant is labelled MISSION VIEJO OR LA PAZ.

FIG-08 — USGS 7.5-minute San Juan Capistrano quadrangle, 1948 edition
Date 1948
Repository U.S. Geological Survey
Rights Public domain (U.S. Government work).
Original 2336x2400 — research/historical_imagery/01_topo_1948_footprint.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-08.jpg (1752x1800)

Interpretation boundary. The earliest topographic sheet covering the footprint. Note that USGS symbology has no dipping-vat symbol, so absence of a vat on this sheet carries little evidential weight.

FIG-09 — USGS orthophoto quadrangle, 1974
Date 1974
Repository U.S. Geological Survey
Rights Public domain (U.S. Government work).
Original 2337x2400 — research/historical_imagery/04_aerial_1974_footprint.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-09.jpg (1753x1800)

Interpretation boundary. Still open rangeland with dirt tracks and the riparian corridor. Mission Viejo's build-out is visible to the west.

FIG-10 — One tile from the systematic survey grid
Date 1937-1938
Repository OC Survey Geospatial Services, County of Orange
Rights Public record, County of Orange. Retrieved from the county's public ArcGIS image service.
Original 2038x1810 — research/historical_imagery/oc_aerials/tiles/1937_r1c1.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-10.jpg (1800x1599)

Interpretation boundary. Zone A was divided into a 4x3 grid and each tile examined at full resolution for both the 1929 and 1937-38 frames - 24 tiles in total. Each carries its own corner coordinates and a 200 m scale bar so any reader can return to the same ground. Pencil annotations are original survey marks on the county's working print.

FIG-11 — The ranch structure on the 1948 topographic sheet
Date 1948
Repository U.S. Geological Survey
Rights Public domain (U.S. Government work).
Original 1120x880 — research/historical_imagery/07_zoom_1948_ranch_structure_elev307.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-11.jpg (1120x880)

Interpretation boundary. The single building inside the footprint, at a trail convergence near elevation 307. The map does not label it. Recorded as a ranch-activity node, not as a dip site.

FIG-12 — Why automated detection failed
Date 1948
Repository U.S. Geological Survey
Rights Public domain (U.S. Government work).
Original 920x720 — research/historical_imagery/10_zoom_1948_south_false_positives.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-12.jpg (920x720)

Interpretation boundary. An automated detector returned 25 building-like candidates on the 1948 sheet. Visual review showed 24 were artifacts - letters from the MISSION VIEJO map label, red section-line dots, and a benchmark X. None of the rejected candidates is plotted anywhere in this publication.

FIG-13 — USDA BAI Circular 174, 'Eradicating Cattle Ticks in California' - contents
Date 1911
Repository USDA National Agricultural Library, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (US Government work). Archive.org states: 'The contributing institution believes that this item is not in copyright.'
Original 1621x2725 — evidence/images/usda_bai_circ174_1911_contents_and_list_of_plates.png
Published as assets/images/fig-13.jpg (1071x1800)

Interpretation boundary. A federal circular published specifically about California. Establishes that the programme addressed this state directly. Circular No. 174, reprinted from the 26th Annual Report of the Bureau of Animal Industry, 1909.

FIG-14 — Circular 174, page 285 - Orange County listed as heavily tick-infested
Date 1911
Repository USDA National Agricultural Library, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (US Government work). Archive.org states: 'The contributing institution believes that this item is not in copyright.'
Original 1650x2734 — evidence/images/usda_bai_circ174_1911_p285_ORANGE_and_SAN_DIEGO_counties_heavily_tick_infested.png
Published as assets/images/fig-14.jpg (1086x1800)

Interpretation boundary. THE DECISIVE PAGE. Lists Orange County among counties 'heavily infested', and refers to losses 'south of the quarantine line in California'. This is COUNTY-LEVEL classification only - it does NOT place a dipping vat on any parcel, on Rancho Mission Viejo, or within the Ladera Ranch footprint.

FIG-15 — Circular 174, page 293 - the required vat poison warning
Date 1911
Repository USDA National Agricultural Library, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (US Government work). Archive.org states: 'The contributing institution believes that this item is not in copyright.'
Original 1596x2709 — evidence/images/usda_bai_circ174_1911_p293_VAT_POISON_WARNING_sign_text.png
Published as assets/images/fig-15.jpg (1060x1800)

Interpretation boundary. Verbatim text of the notice USDA required at every vat: 'WARNING! The fluid in this vat is POISONOUS to man and all animals.' Establishes official recognition of the hazard. Says nothing about whether any vat operated on this land.

FIG-16 — Circular 174, figure 7 - swim vat plans and specifications
Date 1911
Repository USDA National Agricultural Library, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (US Government work). Archive.org states: 'The contributing institution believes that this item is not in copyright.'
Original 1621x2721 — evidence/images/usda_bai_circ174_1911_p295_fig7_swim_vat_plans.png
Published as assets/images/fig-16.jpg (1072x1800)

Interpretation boundary. The large permanent design: approximately 2,088 gallons at five feet depth. A structure of this class WOULD leave a photographically obvious footprint - which is why the imagery search retains some force against this design specifically.

FIG-17 — Circular 174, Plate XV - cage vat in operation
Date 1911
Repository USDA National Agricultural Library, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (US Government work). Archive.org states: 'The contributing institution believes that this item is not in copyright.'
Original 2650x1550 — evidence/images/usda_bai_circ174_1911_PLATE_XV_cage_vat_for_dipping_cattle.png
Published as assets/images/fig-17.jpg (1800x1053)

Interpretation boundary. Three photographs: gross view, cage lowered with attendant ducking the animal, and cage raised. Materials cost $55-65. Per page 299 this vat 'is installed near the corrals and requires only a short chute', and draining pens 'are not essential and are rarely used'.

FIG-18 — Circular 174, figure 10 - small wade tank plans
Date 1911
Repository USDA National Agricultural Library, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (US Government work). Archive.org states: 'The contributing institution believes that this item is not in copyright.'
Original 1655x2742 — evidence/images/usda_bai_circ174_1911_p299_fig10_small_wade_tank_plans.png
Published as assets/images/fig-18.jpg (1086x1800)

Interpretation boundary. THE FIGURE THAT FORCED CORRECTION C-004. Materials cost 'less than $10'; roughly 15 ft long and 4 ft deep; buildable 'of either lumber or cement'. An installation of this size need not be resolvable on 1.15 ft/px aerial photography, which downgrades this investigation's negative imagery finding to Low confidence.

FIG-19 — BAI Circular 183, 'Precautions in the use of arsenic'
Date 1911
Repository USDA National Agricultural Library, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (US Government work). Archive.org states: 'The contributing institution believes that this item is not in copyright.'
Original 1613x2705 — evidence/images/usda_bai_circ183_1911_p8_precautions_use_of_arsenic_POISON_WARNING.png
Published as assets/images/fig-19.jpg (1073x1800)

Interpretation boundary. Addresses arsenic absorption through cuts in the skin and by vapour, and warns of livestock and human fatality. Federal documentation that the material was handled as a recognised acute poison.

FIG-20 — BAI Circular 207, figure 1 - concrete dipping vat engineering drawing
Date 1912
Repository USDA National Agricultural Library, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (US Government work). Archive.org states: 'The contributing institution believes that this item is not in copyright.'
Original 2713x1625 — evidence/images/usda_bai_circ207_1912_fig1_concrete_dipping_vat_plan.png
Published as assets/images/fig-20.jpg (1800x1078)

Interpretation boundary. Standardised federal construction plan. Included to show what the permanent design specified; no such structure has been identified on this land.

FIG-21 — Cattle grazing near a stream, Santa Margarita ranch, San Diego County, 1900
Date 1900
Repository USC Libraries Special Collections / California Historical Society Collection, 1860-1960
Rights Public Domain. Credit both 'University of Southern California. Libraries' and 'California Historical Society' as the source. Digitally reproduced by the USC Digital Library.
Original 1200x953 — evidence/images/usc_chs2358_UC139843_cattle_grazing_near_small_stream_santa_margarita_ranch_sd_county_1900.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-21.jpg (1200x953)

Interpretation boundary. CRITICAL BOUNDARY: this depicts the Santa Margarita ranch in SAN DIEGO COUNTY - the Rancho Santa Margarita y Las Flores property, now largely Camp Pendleton. It is NOT the Ladera Ranch footprint. The properties shared ownership under the O'Neill and Flood interests and were partitioned in the 1940s, but they are different land. Included to illustrate the ranching operation of the period, NOT as a depiction of this study area. Repository states the date as 1900, not circa. No photographer is named; do not attribute to C.C. Pierce.

FIG-22 — Herd of cattle grazing, Santa Margarita Ranch, San Diego County, 1900
Date 1900
Repository USC Libraries Special Collections / California Historical Society Collection, 1860-1960
Rights Public Domain. Credit both 'University of Southern California. Libraries' and 'California Historical Society' as the source. Digitally reproduced by the USC Digital Library.
Original 1200x944 — evidence/images/usc_chs2360_UC139844_herd_of_cattle_grazing_santa_margarita_ranch_sd_county_1900.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-22.jpg (1200x944)

Interpretation boundary. Same boundary as FIG-21: SAN DIEGO COUNTY land, not the Ladera Ranch footprint. Shows herd scale in the period during which the tick programme operated. Call no. CHS-2360, DOI 10.25549/chs-m9638.

FIG-23 — Santa Marguerita Ranch - Vineyard and Winery, 1886
Date 1886
Repository USC Libraries Special Collections / California Historical Society Collection, 1860-1960
Rights Public Domain. Credit both 'University of Southern California. Libraries' and 'California Historical Society' as the source. Digitally reproduced by the USC Digital Library.
Original 1200x940 — evidence/images/usc_chs2085_UC123008_santa_marguerita_ranch_vineyard_and_winery_1886.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-23.jpg (1200x940)

Interpretation boundary. DATE CORRECTED: the repository states 1886, not the 1887 commonly cited online, and spells the ranch 'Marguerita'. The 1887 date traces only to an uncredited reproduction with no repository or identifier. SAN DIEGO COUNTY property, not the Ladera Ranch footprint. Call no. CHS-2085; additionally released CC BY 3.0.

FIG-24 — Plat of Rancho Santa Margarita y Las Flores, surveyed by Max Strobel, July 1867
Date 1867
Repository The Bancroft Library, University of California, Berkeley
Rights Bancroft Library digital collections; consult repository for reuse terms.
Original 1500x1155 — evidence/images/Bancroft_LandCaseMapE-1362A_1867_Plat_RanchoSantaMargaritaYLasFlores_Strobel.jpg
Published as assets/images/fig-24.jpg (1500x1155)

Interpretation boundary. NOT the 1912 plat sought. No 1912 plat has been located - see DE-008, and note that BLM GLO Records could not be searched at all (DE-007), so this is an unsearched archive rather than a demonstrated absence. Cartouche states 89,742 acres; handwritten certifications on the sheet are dated 1868 and 1870, later than the survey. SAN DIEGO COUNTY grant, adjoining but distinct from Rancho Mission Viejo. Land Case Map E-1362A.

FIG-25 — Sections infested with Southern cattle fever ticks and quarantined, 30 June 1912
Date 1912
Repository California State Veterinarian / State of California, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (California state government publication, 1912).
Original 2078x3540 — evidence/documents/CAStateVet_1912_p11_TickInfestationMap_June30_1912.png
Published as assets/images/fig-25.jpg (1057x1800)

Interpretation boundary. Dark areas represent infested and quarantined sections. San Diego County is hatched almost entirely. Orange County is essentially clear EXCEPT a small solid wedge at its extreme southern coastal corner where it adjoins San Diego County. STATE-SCALE MAP: county outlines are generalised and the drafting cannot resolve a four-square-mile community. It shows the residual area's approximate position, not its boundary.

FIG-26 — The same map, Orange / San Diego County detail
Date 1912
Repository California State Veterinarian / State of California, via Internet Archive
Rights Public domain (California state government publication, 1912).
Original 2492x2264 — evidence/documents/zoom_1912map_OrangeSanDiego.png
Published as assets/images/fig-26.jpg (1800x1635)

Interpretation boundary. The 'ORANGE' label sits well north-west of the solid dark wedge. The wedge lies at the coastal corner adjoining San Diego County - the San Clemente / San Onofre area. Ladera Ranch is roughly 8-10 miles north of that corner and appears OUTSIDE the residual quarantine area. THIS IS A READING OF A SMALL-SCALE MAP, NOT A BOUNDARY DETERMINATION. The proclamation text itself does not delimit the area.

Source Library

The bibliography lists sources. This lists where the underlying material actually is — which documents were retrieved and archived locally, which were read, and which are known to exist but could not be obtained.

A citation to a document nobody opened is a weaker thing than a citation to one that was read, and this report distinguishes them.

Appendices

Appendix A — Reproducing this investigation

Every quantitative claim can be regenerated from the repository.

Script Produces
pipelines/python/extract_topo_water.py The 41-body surface-water layer from the 1968 sheet
pipelines/python/premise_homes_on_water.py The water-proximity test and its null
pipelines/python/premise_water_and_homes.py Terrain and drainage analysis (see the exclusion in Ch. 22)
pipelines/python/detect_water_1930s.py The abandoned detector. Retained with its failure documented in the docstring so it is not naively retried
pipelines/python/build_geojson.py All published GIS layers
scripts/build_publication.py This publication from the chapter markdown
scripts/build_backmatter.py Bibliography, image credits, evidence-category chapters, source library, version history — all from the data files
scripts/prepare_pub_assets.py Figure derivatives and the image archive
scripts/build_pdf.sh Print-quality PDF
tests/test_data_integrity.py Privacy and provenance checks (7 tests)

Appendix B — The integrity tests

These run against the data, not the prose, and they encode the project's non-negotiable rules:

  • test_health_events_have_no_address_or_coordinate — no health record may carry a location
  • test_health_events_aggregate_columns_only — aggregate level only
  • test_every_source_has_valid_grade — no ungraded source
  • test_records_reference_existing_sources — no claim without a registered source
  • test_geojson_layers_valid — published geometry parses
  • test_application_events_have_valid_evidence_class — no unclassified assertion
  • test_no_bare_likely_application_events — no inferred record published without a confidence marker

Appendix C — Common misreadings, corrected in advance

Misreading What the report actually says
"They found a dipping vat at Ladera Ranch" No vat has been found anywhere. The nearest documented 1908 dipping was at Capistrano, 3.4 miles away
"Trabuco Canyon dipping proves it happened here" Trabuco Canyon is 8.4 miles away and outside Zone B. It shares a drainage name, not a location
"The county never studied the land" A Phase I assessment was performed. Its historical review began in 1952. That is a window gap, not a failure
"Arsenic was found in Ladera Ranch soil" No soil measurement from the residential footprint exists. Arsenic findings are from school sites on former agricultural land nearby
"The homes were built on the old cattle grounds" Tested and not supported — 0.97×, p = 0.51
"No contaminants were found at Oso Grande" Correct, and it means none were looked for in soil. The site was cleared documentarily
"This links cattle dipping to the cancer cases" Nothing in this report bears on the illnesses. No causal claim is made or supported

Appendix D — A note on structure

The evidence matrix is rendered as a searchable, filterable section following the final chapter, generated directly from EVIDENCE_MATRIX.csv. Chapter 24 introduces it; Chapters 25–28 group the same claims by classification.

Chapters 16–19 are deliberately layered rather than sequential. Ch. 16 is the audit overview and method; Ch. 17 examines the 1952 source window and the standard governing it; Ch. 18 records what the review did cover; Ch. 19 records what does not appear in it. Reading 16 alone gives the finding. Reading 17–19 gives the basis for it.

Chapters 29 and 30 split archival from physical work, because the sequencing between them is itself a recommendation: archives first, ground second. This project learned that the hard way — see Ch. 8 §8.8.

Appendix E — Contact and contribution

See the invitation at the front of this report.

Materials welcome: documents, photographs, maps, surveys, ranch records, recollections, and corrections.

Not accepted: medical information about any individual, residential addresses, or anything identifying a specific child or household. This is a permanent constraint, not a preference.

Chapter 37

Version History

This project issues corrections openly and in full. Nothing is silently revised. Where a finding changed, the superseded reasoning is retained in the text alongside the correction, because a report that shows only what survived scrutiny is not an honest report.

Version 1.0 — 39 catalogued claims, 91 registered sources.

Corrections issued

ID Correction Date
C-001 Study-area centroid was ~1.93 miles too far north 2026-07-18
C-002 Creek misidentified in the historical-imagery analysis 2026-07-18
C-003 "Earliest available imagery is 1948" was wrong 2026-07-18
C-004 The "a corral complex would be unmistakable" claim was overconfident 2026-07-19
C-005 The 1842 grant date circulating online is wrong 2026-07-19
C-006 Armor (1921) was briefly treated as a land-title source 2026-07-19
C-007 The Ladera Ranch footprint is NOT entirely unsectioned land 2026-07-20
C-008 The survey underlying the patent is 1858, not 1867 2026-07-20
C-009 "No dip chemistry is named for Orange County" is superseded 2026-07-20

Full text of every correction, including what was affected and what was not, is retained in research/CORRECTIONS.md.

The pattern in these corrections

Four corrections have been issued. Three of them (C-001, C-003, C-004) share a shape worth naming: an assumption about the physical evidence was adopted without checking it, and the assumption happened to make the analysis simpler.

  • C-001 — a centroid was inherited from a brief and never verified. It was 1.93 miles wrong, and correcting it moved two plugged wells from ~2 miles away to ~0.25 and ~0.77 miles.
  • C-003 — the earliest imagery was assumed to be 1948, which conveniently made a dip vat undetectable in principle. Photography from 1929 existed the whole time.
  • C-004 — having obtained that photography, the project then assumed the target would be large and declared the resolution objection resolved. USDA's own California circular documents wade tanks costing under ten dollars.

Each was caught by going to a primary source or by rendering the data rather than trusting a table. That is the only countermeasure this project has found that works.

Evidence Matrix

Every substantive claim in this investigation, with its classification, confidence, supporting sources, and — mandatorily — its limiting or contradictory evidence. Select a claim to expand it. The counter-evidence field is never left blank: where a claim has no meaningful counter-evidence, that is stated explicitly.

EM-001 Ladera Ranch sits on land that was part of the Rancho Mission Viejo cattle operation prior to development ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
USGS 7.5' quadrangles 1948-1968 label the area MISSION VIEJO OR LA PAZ; OC Survey aerials 1929-1946 show open rangeland with stock trails and grazing patterns
Limiting or contradictory evidence
None identified
Citation
USGS San Juan Capistrano 7.5' quadrangle, 1948 and 1968 editions; OC Survey Historic_Imagery_v2 frames OID 346/310/293
Status
Verified · added 2026-07-18
EM-002 The Ladera Ranch footprint was undeveloped open rangeland in every available pre-1950 aerial photograph ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
Six pre-1950 frames examined (1929/1931/1937-38/1938/1946-47); systematic 4x3 tile survey of Zone A at full resolution; no structures resolvable inside Zone A except one node on Trabuco Creek
Limiting or contradictory evidence
1946-47 frame has a coverage gap over the NW quarter of the footprint
Citation
OC Survey Historic_Imagery_v2, frames OID 346/351/310/340/286/293; research/historical_imagery/README.md
Status
Verified · added 2026-07-18
EM-003 No cattle dip vat or corral complex has been identified anywhere inside the Ladera Ranch footprint ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence Low
Supporting evidence
Systematic tile survey of Zone A on the 1937-38 frame at 1.15 ft/px, a resolution at which a corral would be unmistakable; also examined 1929, 1946-47, 1948/1968/1974 topographic and orthophoto sheets
Limiting or contradictory evidence
SUBSTANTIALLY REVISED 2026-07-19, see correction C-004. USDA Circular 174 documents that California vats were frequently SMALL: a cage vat cost $55-65 in materials and 'is installed near the corrals and requires only a short chute'; for cage vats 'draining pens are not essential and are rarely used'. A wade tank cost 'less than $10 for materials' and measures roughly 15 ft long by 4 ft deep. Structures of that class need not produce a large corral complex and may not be resolvable at 1.15 ft/px. The earlier claim that 'a corral complex would be unmistakable' was overconfident. PLUS the original limits: imagery begins 1929, twelve years after the programme; vats were commonly backfilled; USGS symbology had no vat symbol.
Citation
research/historical_imagery/README.md sections 3 and 5
Status
Verified - confidence downgraded · added 2026-07-18

Supporting evidence
Raster catalog query returned 36 frames intersecting the footprint spanning 1929-1997; six pre-date 1950; frames retrieved and georeferencing verified against modern landmarks
Limiting or contradictory evidence

EM-006 Every one of the 41 ranch-era water bodies now has a modern building within 500 metres; median distance to the nearest building is 66 metres ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
Measured against 10618 OSM building footprints; 24/41 within 100 m, 34/41 within 200 m, 40/41 within 300 m, 41/41 within 500 m
Limiting or contradictory evidence
OSM completeness is not guaranteed uniform, though under-mapping would bias distances upward not downward, so the finding is conservative

Verified · added 2026-07-18
EM-007 Modern housing was NOT preferentially sited on the ranch-era water bodies ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
Zone-A-restricted null: water sites average 9.0 buildings within 100 m vs 9.3 for 6000 random points in the same area. Enrichment 0.97x, permutation p = 0.51
Limiting or contradictory evidence
A bbox-wide null gave 0.79x, inflated by dense Mission Viejo inside the extent; the Zone-A-restricted null is the fair comparison and is reported as primary
Citation
research/historical_imagery/premise_null_zonea.json
Status
Verified · added 2026-07-18
EM-008 Modern slope beneath buildings cannot be used to infer original landform siting ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
Buildings sit at median 1.7 degrees vs 6.7 degrees for available land, but Ladera Ranch was mass-graded, so pad flatness was manufactured by the grading under investigation. The comparison is circular.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
None - this is a methodological exclusion, deliberately recorded so the figure is not later mistaken for a finding

research/historical_imagery/README.md section 6; USGS 3DEP DEM

EM-011 Orange County was within the California cattle tick quarantine area and was classified by the USDA as heavily tick-infested ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
USDA Bureau of Animal Industry Circular 174 (1911), p.285, lists Orange County among counties found 'heavily infested' with cattle ticks, alongside San Luis Obispo, Santa Barbara, San Diego, Fresno and Ventura. The same page refers to losses 'south of the quarantine line in California'. Page read and verified directly, not via summary. Circular 174 is titled 'Eradicating Cattle Ticks in California' and is a US Government work.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
Reported at COUNTY level only. It does not place a dipping vat on any specific parcel, on Rancho Mission Viejo, or within the Ladera Ranch footprint. Orange County in 1911 covered roughly 800 square miles. This establishes the programme's geographic scope, not any site-specific activity.
Citation
MacKellar, W.M. and Hart, G.H. (1911) 'Eradicating Cattle Ticks in California', USDA Bureau of Animal Industry Circular No. 174, p.285. archive.org/details/CAT31283802
Status
Verified · added 2026-07-19
EM-012 Arsenical cattle dipping actually occurred within the Ladera Ranch footprint OPEN
Classification
OPEN QUESTION · confidence None
Supporting evidence
No direct evidence. Terrain logic places working facilities near water and trail convergences, and one such node exists.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
No vat found on adequate imagery. No documentary record located. No soil sampling has tested for it in the residential footprint. This is an UNPROVEN HYPOTHESIS and must not be presented as more.
Citation
No supporting citation exists - this is the central open question of the investigation
Status
OPEN · added 2026-07-18
EM-013 DTSC found arsenic in soil at several school sites on former agricultural land near Ladera Ranch ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
EnviroStor records for Carl Hankey Elementary, Plant Depot School Site, and San Juan Elementary list arsenic among contaminants found
Limiting or contradictory evidence
Arsenic occurs naturally in California soils at background levels; presence alone does not establish an anthropogenic source or a link to cattle dipping. Former orchard land also carries lead arsenate residues from a completely different practice.
Citation
DTSC EnviroStor site records
Status
Verified · added 2026-07-18

EM-015 USDA-specified California dipping vats included small, low-cost designs that would not necessarily produce a large or photographically distinctive surface footprint HISTORICAL
Classification
HISTORICAL CONTEXT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
USDA BAI Circular 174 (1911) p.299: cage vat materials cost $55-65 and the vat 'is installed near the corrals and requires only a short chute'; 'Draining pens with such vats are not essential and are rarely used, as the animal drains while the cage is being raised.' The wade tank 'can be constructed with an outlay of less than $10 for materials' and is 'recommended only in the disinfection of very small herds'. Figure 10 shows a vertical section approximately 15 ft long and 4 ft deep. Pages read directly.
Limiting or contradictory evidence

Circulars 183 (1911) and 207 (1912) each carry a section headed 'Precautions in the use of arsenic' addressing absorption through cuts and vapour, and warning of livestock and human fatality.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
This establishes that the practice was recognised as hazardous and was federally documented. It says nothing about whether any such vat operated on this land, nor about the fate of any residue.

EM-017 Photographs of Santa Margarita Ranch cattle operations circa 1900 exist, but depict San Diego County land, not the Ladera Ranch footprint HISTORICAL
Classification
HISTORICAL CONTEXT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
Four photographs acquired from USC Digital Library / California Historical Society Collection: CHS-2358, CHS-2359, CHS-2360 (all dated 1900) and CHS-2085 (dated 1886). All are public domain.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
CRITICAL INTERPRETATION BOUNDARY: the repository titles place these on the Santa Margarita ranch in SAN DIEGO COUNTY - the Rancho Santa Margarita y Las Flores property, now largely Camp Pendleton. That is NOT the Ladera Ranch footprint. The properties shared ownership under the O'Neill and Flood interests and were partitioned in the 1940s, but they are different land. REFINES AND NARROWS the '1907-1917' window this project had assumed. That figure was a general California/national approximation; the Orange-County-specific record indicates roughly 1908-1912. The primary instruments are held by the California State Library and could not be retrieved (DE-010). A narrower window means a longer gap to the 1929 imagery: 17 years, not 12.

Pesticides addressed qualitatively: 'pesticides and/or herbicides are not suspected within the near-surface soils beneath the site. Any pesticides and/or herbicides at the site were likely mixed and/or placed at lower depths during the development of the site.' DTSC required a PEA 8 Sep 2003, then on 7 Oct 2003 accepted the Addendum and concluded 'further environmental investigation of the site is not required', based on a site visit and geotechnical borings reviewed for methane and organics. Limiting or contradictory evidence
This is a legitimate outcome under the applicable process, and a Phase I ESA is non-sampling by definition under ASTM 1527 - so absence of sampling is a property of the instrument, not a finding about the soil. 'No contaminants found' means none were looked for in soil. Tract-level grading and geotechnical reports, and any developer-commissioned sampling for the residential areas, are not in the public repositories searched and may exist.

EM-026 Rancho Mission Vieja or La Paz was granted 4 April 1845 by Governor Pio Pico to Agustin Olvera, contained 46,432.65 acres, and was patented 6 August 1866 ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
PRIMARY SOURCE: Ogden Hoffman, Reports of Land Cases Determined in the U.S. District Court, Northern District of California (1862), Appendix claim no.396, verbatim: 'Juan Foster, claimant for Mission Vieja or La Paz, in Los Angeles county, granted April 4th, 1845, by Pio Pico to Agustin Olvera; claim filed October 16th, 1852, confirmed by the Commission October 31st, 1854, by the District Court February 21st, 1857, and appeal dismissed June 4th, 1857; containing 46,432.65 acres.' READ AND VERIFIED DIRECTLY from full text, corroborated by the volume's own index. Acreage independently matched by Lewis (1890) p.825, Armor (1921) p.36, and the California Secretary of State US Surveyor General grant-map list, which also gives the patent date 6 Aug 1866.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
FORSTER WAS THE CLAIMANT, NOT THE GRANTEE - the grantee was Agustin Olvera; several secondary accounts collapse this. County given as Los Angeles; Orange County was not created until 1889. Acreage is the CONFIRMED figure and need not match the original Mexican grant description. A grant date of 1842 circulating online is contradicted by this record - see C-005. Whether the Ladera Ranch footprint fell within Mission Viejo, within Trabuco (a separate grant, 22,184.47 acres), or across the boundary is NOT resolved.
Citation
Hoffman, Ogden (1862), Reports of Land Cases, Appendix p.55 claim 396. archive.org/details/reportsoflandcas01hoff
Status
Verified · added 2026-07-19

Supporting evidence

Passage read and verified directly.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
SEE CORRECTION C-006. Armor contains substantive errors in this very passage: he calls the Mexican grants 'Spanish', states Mission Viejo 'originally belonged to the Picos' (Hoffman gives Agustin Olvera), and elsewhere conflates San Juan Cajon de Santa Ana with Canon de Santa Ana. THE CHARLES CROCKER LINK IS UNCORROBORATED and appears in no other source consulted. Secondary accounts of the O'Neill/Flood transaction conflict on year (1882 vs 1884), price ($457,000 vs ~$460,000), structure (equal ownership vs Flood-owned), and the half-interest date (1906 vs 1907). All are grade B2 or C. The primary record is a deed, which - Orange County not existing until 1889 - would sit in Los Angeles and San Diego County records.
Citation
Armor (1921), biographical sketch of John O. Forster

EM-035 Arsenical dip was the standard California cattle dipping solution from 1907, and its formula is documented ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908), read directly: 'The arsenical dip was first used in California during 1907, and such good results were obtained that it has been our most effective remedy ever since.' The formula given: Arsenic 8 pounds, Carbonate of soda 24 pounds, Soap 24 pounds, Pine tar 1 gallon, Water 500 gallons. The report adds: 'This solution, being poisonous in nature, is carefully used in order that cattle dipped in same are not poisoned themselves.'
Limiting or contradictory evidence
Statewide practice, not a site-specific finding. It establishes WHAT was used in California from 1907 onward. It does not establish that any vat existed on Rancho Mission Viejo or within the Ladera Ranch footprint.
Citation
California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908), p.10; archived at evidence/documents/
Status
Verified · added 2026-07-20
EM-036 Orange County cattle were dipped in the arsenical solution, in a swim vat, in the 1907-1908 period ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908), read directly: 'One notable case occurred in Orange County, where a number of cattle were dipped in this solution preparatory to moving them on to a tick free pasture. Subsequently several head of these cattle died from what was claimed to be arsenical poisoning, due to the cattle drinking the dip while swimming through the vat.' 'This solution' refers to the arsenical dip whose formula is given on the same page. 'SWIMMING THROUGH THE VAT' indicates a SWIM VAT - the large permanent design, not a cage vat or wade tank.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
The State Veterinarian DISPUTED the arsenical-poisoning claim: 'Upon autopsy not the slightest indication of arsenical poisoning could be found.' He attributed the deaths to exhaustion, the cattle having been 'driven a distance of some 30 to 35 miles' on the day they were dipped. This is an OFFICIAL COUNTER-NARRATIVE to the newspaper account of the Levengood claim and the two should be read together. CRITICALLY: the vat is placed in 'Orange County' with NO location given. It is not attributed to Rancho Mission Viejo and not placed within the Ladera Ranch footprint.
Citation
California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908), pp.10-11; archived at evidence/documents/
Status
Verified · added 2026-07-20
EM-037 Cattle were driven 30 to 35 miles in a single day to reach dipping facilities in Orange County ESTABLISHED
Classification

Supporting evidence
California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908): the cattle in the Orange County incident 'were driven a distance of some 30 to 35 miles' on the day they were dipped.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
A single documented instance, not a general practice statement. It CUTS BOTH WAYS for this investigation: it shows cattle could be dipped very far from their home range, which REDUCES the necessity of an on-ranch vat - but equally shows cattle from a wide catchment converged on whichever facilities existed.

EM-038 The Orange County quarantine was not fully lifted in March 1912; a small area in the extreme southwestern part of the county remained quarantined ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
California State Veterinarian, Sixth Biennial Report (1912), read directly: 'March 7, 1912, by proclamation, your Excellency removed from quarantine all of Orange County EXCEPT A SMALL AREA IN THE EXTREME SOUTHWESTERN PART of said county. This leaves at the end of June 30, 1912, only San Diego County, that part of San Luis Obispo County lying west of...' THE MAP HAS NOW BEEN EXAMINED (p.11, rendered at 500 dpi and read directly). It shows San Diego County hatched almost entirely, and Orange County essentially clear EXCEPT a small solid wedge at its extreme southern coastal corner where it adjoins San Diego County - the San Clemente / San Onofre area.
Limiting or contradictory evidence
The residual area is NOT delimited in the proclamation text. The map is STATE-SCALE: county outlines are generalised and the drafting cannot resolve a four-square-mile community. Reading the wedge as the San Clemente/San Onofre coastal corner is an INTERPRETATION OF A SMALL-SCALE MAP, not a boundary determination. On that reading Ladera Ranch, roughly 8-10 miles north, falls OUTSIDE the residual area - but the map cannot prove it, and the underlying 7 March 1912 proclamation has NOT been obtained. Note also that the adjoining San Diego County land was Rancho Santa Margarita y Las Flores, under the same O'Neill/Flood ownership as Rancho Mission Viejo.
Citation
California State Veterinarian, Sixth Biennial Report (1912); archived at evidence/documents/
Status
Verified - map examined; boundary indicative, not determinative · added 2026-07-20
EM-039 Orange County was one of only three counties under strict quarantine in California in 1908 ESTABLISHED
Classification
ESTABLISHED FACT · confidence High
Supporting evidence
California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908): 'To sum up: San Diego, Imperial and Orange counties are at present the only strictly quarantined counties left in the state.'
Limiting or contradictory evidence
A statement about regulatory status at one date, at county level. It indicates the tick problem in Orange County was among the most severe in California, but places no facility anywhere.
Citation
California State Veterinarian, Fifth Biennial Report (1908)
Status
Verified · added 2026-07-20
